Title: Sharp Limits of Graph-Based Nearest Neighbor Search on the Sphere

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07603v1

Published Time: Fri, 06 Nov 2026 02:17:37 GMT

Number of Pages: 86

Markdown Content:
# Sharp Limits of Graph-Based Nearest Neighbor Search on the Sphere 

Kejing Lu Department of Electrical and Electronic Engineering University of Yamanashi, Japan 

k.riku@yamanashi.ac.jp 

Abstract 

We study the degree needed for greedy graph search to find the exact nearest neighbor from every starting point on graphs of n independent data points on the unit sphere in Rd. For uniform data, we determine an explicit sublinear degree threshold when d is proportional to log n, and the threshold 2d/ 2+ o(d) in the dense regime √log n ≪ d ≪ log n. Above these thresholds by fixed exponent gaps, the nearest-neighbor graph succeeds from every entry for most uniform queries with high probability, with evaluation cost on the same exponential scale as its degree. Matching lower bounds hold for every graph designed after observing the sample. For nonuniform data in the dense regime, local density variation shifts the critical degree. Under explicit local regularity conditions and within a specified range of density variation, we derive a sharp worst-case degree curve and show that the nearest-neighbor graph attains it. We construct sampling densities and query distributions for which, with high probability, every graph whose degree lies below this curve by a fixed exponent gap has failing entries for most queries. Allowing fresh uniform random entries reduces the cost of exact recovery. For uniform data in the dense regime, we determine the exact exponent for the number of distinct distance evaluations over a range of degrees below the critical threshold. Independent restarts on a single graph joining points within a prescribed radius attain this exponent with high probability. Matching lower bounds hold for adaptive local exploration on threshold and nearest-neighbor graphs using entries independent of the data. Optimizing within this degree range lowers the exponent from 12 log 2 ≈ 0.347 for success from every entry to approximately 0 .243. arXiv:2611.07603v1 [cs.DS] 5 Nov 2026 Contents

1.1 Graph navigation and the every-entry question 

Approximate nearest-neighbor search (ANNS) is a fundamental problem in data mining and machine learning. To support high-performance search at scale, many real-world systems currently build their ANNS indices from proximity graphs. Among the most prominent examples are HNSW [MY20], NSG [FXWC19], and DiskANN [SDS +19]. Although these indices use different graph structures, their common navigation principle has its algorithmic roots in Kleinberg’s seminal work on the small-world phenomenon [Kle00]. In Kleinberg’s model, decentralized routing moves a message toward a known target node by repeatedly forwarding it to the available contact closest to that target. Proximity-graph search transfers this principle from a target node in the network to an external query. Specifically, a navigation graph G assigns a short neighbor list G(x) to each data point x. Its degree is ∆( G) = max x |G(x)|. We use ∆ for a chosen list size or degree bound. Starting from an entry point, greedy search repeatedly moves to the listed neighbor closest to the query and stops when none is closer. We measure its evaluation cost by the number of distinct data points whose distance to the query is evaluated. Despite their widespread use and strong empirical performance, proximity-graph indices are not yet supported by a comparably complete theory. Existing average-case analyses have made important progress by studying navigation from a random or otherwise prescribed entry point on specific graph constructions. Laarhoven [Laa18] analyzed random restarts on near-neighbor graphs over random spherical data and asked whether matching lower bounds for graph search could be established. Building on this line of work, Prokhorenkova and Shekhovtsov [PS20] proved, for log log n ≪ d ≪ log n and queries sampled near a designated nearest neighbor, that a threshold graph succeeds from their prescribed random start when its radius exceeds √2 times the typical nearest-neighbor distance. They also showed how adding edges between distant points and retaining several candidates during search can reduce its cost. Together, these works provide the main quantitative foundations for graph navigation on random spherical data. These analyses, however, focus on specified graph constructions and prescribed entry rules, so they do not determine whether the degree scale they obtain is intrinsic to graph navigation or can be improved by a graph designed after observing the sample. Moreover, existing analyses almost exclusively assume uniform sampling. Under nonuniform sampling, it therefore remains unknown how local density variation changes the degree required for exact navigation from every entry and whether the resulting degree is unavoidable for every graph. Before discussing how to tackle these issues in more detail, we describe our setting. In this paper, we work on the unit sphere because cosine similarity is widely used in embedding-based retrieval to identify texts or images with similar semantic content. Moreover, the sphere is compact and has no boundary, which allows density variation to be studied without additional tail or boundary conditions. Within this domain, we draw the data independently from a probability density rather than consider arbitrary point sets since, without assumptions on the data, sublinear guarantees fail even for popular implementations. Indyk and Xu [IX23] exhibited inputs on which HNSW, NSG, and the fast-preprocessing variant of DiskANN require empirical query time linear in the data-set size to achieve reasonable accuracy. Random sampling therefore provides a natural setting for determining when sublinear navigation is possible. Notably, even within this distributional setting, the behavior of greedy search depends on how its entry point is chosen. Laarhoven studied random restarts [Laa18]. Prokhorenkova and Shekhovtsov considered a single random entry [PS20]. However, practical systems use other rules. For example, NSG starts every search from a Navigating Node fixed during construction [FXWC19], whereas 1HNSW enters its base graph only after navigating its upper layers. The resulting base-layer entry is thus query-dependent and may already lie close to the query. We therefore first study when greedy search succeeds from every entry, since an every-entry guarantee covers all such entry rules at once and reveals what the stored graph alone can achieve. We later examine how the answer changes when either exact recovery or success from every entry is relaxed. In this setting, our central question is the following. 

For a data set of n random points on the unit sphere Sd−1 ⊂ Rd, can greedy search find the exact nearest neighbor from every entry with sublinear evaluation cost? If so, what degree and evaluation cost are necessary and sufficient, and can a navigation graph designed after observing the complete data set improve either bound? 

Before answering this question, we fix the asymptotic setting. We let n → ∞ and allow 

d = d(n) → ∞ . A statement holds with high probability if its probability over the random data set is 1 − o(1). It holds for most queries if the set of queries for which it holds has probability 1 − o(1) under the query distribution. Unless stated otherwise, queries are uniform on Sd−1. We distinguish two regimes according to how d grows relative to log n, where log denotes the natural logarithm. We call d = ⌊c log n⌋ for a fixed c > 0 the logarithmic regime and call √log n ≪ d ≪ log n the 

dense regime . For uniform data, the nearest-neighbor distance stays bounded away from zero in the logarithmic regime and tends to zero in the dense regime. Together, these regimes capture the transition from a constant nearest-neighbor distance to a vanishing one while the degree remains the leading scale of the evaluation cost. As c grows, the degree threshold in the logarithmic regime approaches linear in n. At the other end, when d = O(√log n), reaching the nearest neighbor from a distant entry already requires moves on the leading exponential scale if edges have length comparable to the nearest-neighbor distance. Therefore, we focus on the logarithmic and dense regimes in this paper. With the conventions above, our answer to the central question is as follows. For uniform density, the answer is yes, and the threshold is sharp. The degree and evaluation cost are nβ∗(c)±o(1) in the logarithmic regime, where β∗(c) = c 

> 2

log(2 − e−2/c ) < 1, and 2 d/ 2±o(d) in the dense regime. The graph that stores the ∆ nearest neighbors of each point achieves these bounds from every entry for most queries. Every graph of smaller degree has failing entries for most queries, even if it is designed after observing the complete sample. In the dense regime, the same dichotomy extends to a broad class of nonuniform densities along a sharp worst-case curve determined by their local variation. Above the curve, the nearest-neighbor graph still succeeds, with evaluation cost on the same exponential scale as its degree. Below the curve, there exist a density with the prescribed variation and a query distribution spread across the sphere for which every graph of smaller degree fails in the same sense. By contrast, in the logarithmic regime the answer depends on the shape of the density. When the density is at most eo(d) times the uniform density, a suitable graph handles regions of arbitrarily low or zero density. Certain concentrations of high density, however, raise the critical degree for every graph. 

1.2 Model and the critical curve 

We now specify the model and notation needed to explain the critical curve. Write Dn = ( X1, . . . , X n), where the data points are independent with common distribution P = f dσ d−1. Here σd−1 is normalized surface measure on Sd−1 and f is the sampling density. We use the sample indices as data labels and identify each label with its point, since the points are almost surely distinct. For a measurable set A ⊆ Sd−1, the probability that a data point lies in A is P (A) = R 

> A

f dσ d−1. A 2query q ∼ Q is independent of Dn, and Q is fixed before the data are sampled. Both the sampling and query distributions may depend on n. We compare points using geodesic distance δ(·, ·). To describe neighborhoods on the sphere, write C(x, θ ) for the closed spherical cap of radius θ centered at x. Its normalized surface measure is Fd−1(θ) = σd−1(C(x, θ )), independent of x by rotational symmetry. For a fixed integer k ≥ 1, the target points form the set Top k(q) of the k data points nearest to q, so k = 1 gives exact nearest-neighbor search. After observing Dn, one may choose any navigation graph G, where each G(x) is a neighbor list of distinct points in Dn \ { x}. We write dG(x) = |G(x)| for its length. A navigation run from an entry x0 first evaluates x0. At each visited point x, it expands x by scanning the entire list 

G(x) and evaluating the distance to q of each point not already cached. Success is recorded after a completed expansion if the evaluated points include a member of Top k(q), even when the entry itself is a target. Otherwise, the run moves to the listed point closest to q if it is strictly closer than x, and stops if no listed point is closer. We write WG(q; x0) for the number of distinct points evaluated through the first completed expansion after which a target has been evaluated, including the entry itself, and set WG(q; x0) = ∞ if the run stops without evaluating a target. The graph G

succeeds from every entry for q if WG(q; x0) < ∞ for every x0 ∈ Dn. A guarantee from every entry for most queries means that, with probability 1 − o(1) over the data, there is a set of queries of 

Q-measure 1 − o(1) on which G succeeds from every entry. We measure storage by P 

> x∈Dn

|G(x)|,and average degree by n−1 P 

> x∈Dn

|G(x)|. Notably, evaluation cost is different from the number of expansions because one expansion scans an entire neighbor list and different lists may overlap. Across restarts, the cost counts the union of all evaluated points, including evaluations made by unsuccessful runs, so repeated evaluations are charged only once. We apply these measures to two canonical graph families. The threshold graph Gρ lists all other data points within distance ρ of each data point, while the nearest-neighbor graph N∆ lists the ∆ nearest other data points. The uniform upper bound also applies to graphs that retain all edges within a slightly smaller radius and have degree at most 2∆ (see Theorem 3.1(a)). We now turn from the navigation model to the geometry that determines the critical degree. We begin with uniform data and give the geometric intuition behind the threshold. Let θ1 satisfy 

nF d−1(θ1) = 1. A cap of radius θ1 contains one data point in expectation, so θ1 gives the typical exact nearest-neighbor scale. When the current point x has distance θ = δ(q, x ) from q, an improving neighbor on Gρ must lie in C(x, ρ ) ∩ C(q, θ ). We call this intersection the lens . Comparing the surface measure of the lens with Fd−1(θ) gives the fraction of improving locations available within radius ρ. For 0 < θ, ρ < π/ 2, Theorem 2.2 shows that the exponential loss in this fraction vanishes when cos ρ ≤ cos 2 θ. Exact recovery requires enough improving neighbors down to the scale θ1, so we evaluate the boundary at θ1. Since nF d−1(ρ) gives the leading scale of neighbor-list length on 

Gρ, we define the critical radius ρ∗ and the corresponding critical degree ∆ ∗ by cos ρ∗ = cos 2 θ1 and ∆∗ = nF d−1(ρ∗). (1) The first equality in (1) gives sin ρ∗/ sin θ1 = √1 + cos 2 θ1. Since θ1 → 0 in the dense regime, the critical radius satisfies ρ∗ = ( √2 + o(1)) θ1. To compute the critical degree, we use nF d−1(θ1) = 1 to write ∆ ∗ = Fd−1(ρ∗)/F d−1(θ1). Applying the spherical cap asymptotics from Theorem 2.1 gives exponent β∗(c) in the logarithmic regime and γc = 12 log 2 in the dense regime. In the two regimes, the critical degree therefore satisfies ∆∗ =

(nβ∗(c)+ o(1) , d = ⌊c log n⌋,e(γc+o(1)) d = 2 d/ 2+ o(d), √log n ≪ d ≪ log n. (2) Thus the constant √2 and the exponent β∗(c) both follow from the same spherical identity. 3For nonuniform data in the dense regime, the nearest-neighbor scale and the probability of finding a point in a lens depend on location. For each x ∈ Sd−1, let ρ(x) be the smallest radius whose cap has probability at least 1 /n under P . Continuity of cap probability in the radius gives 

nP (C(x, ρ (x))) = 1, so ρ(x) is the local nearest-neighbor scale. We also write ρf (x) when the dependence on the density needs to be explicit. To keep the number of complete expansions subexponential in d, we assume that ρmin := inf x∈Sd−1 ρ(x) satisfies ρmin = e−o(d). For a candidate degree ∆ = eγd with fixed γ > 0, fix 0 ≤ κ ≤ ¯κ < γ and choose a sufficiently large constant factor depending on γ, κ, and ¯ κ. We consider queries q for which the ratio between the essential upper and lower bounds of f within the cap centered at q whose radius is this factor times ρ(q) is at most 

eκd . This condition controls the improving neighbors once the current point is near q. To guarantee progress from every entry before the run reaches this neighborhood, we require the corresponding density ratio to be at most e¯κd within the cap centered at x whose radius is the same factor times 

ρ(x), for every x ∈ Sd−1. The essential bounds disregard sets of surface measure zero. The precise radius and density conditions appear in Section 2.5 and Theorem 4.3. To determine the worst-case critical degree, impose the density bound with parameter κ at every center, with a sufficiently large fixed radius multiplier independent of the degree being tested. Write ∆∗(κ) for the critical degree over these densities satisfying the bound on ρmin . For 0 ≤ κ ≤ 12 log 32 ,our matching upper and lower bounds give ∆∗(κ) =  4e2κ − 2d/ 2+ o(d). (3) At κ = 0, (3) recovers the dense uniform scale in (2). For arbitrarily fixed κ in this range, let ∆ = eγd and let Q be any query distribution with a density on the sphere, fixed before sampling. If e2γ > 4e2κ − 2, then under the stated bound on ρmin and the density bound with parameter ¯κ at every center, with probability 1 − e−Ω( d) over the sample, N∆ succeeds from every entry at every query satisfying the local density bound with parameter κ, outside an exceptional set of 

Q-probability e−Ω( d). Conversely, if e2γ < 4e2κ − 2, we construct a density with the prescribed variation and a query distribution spread across the sphere for which, with probability 1 − o(1) over the sample, every graph of degree at most ∆ has failing entries for most queries. Taking ¯ κ = κ gives the density class in (3), over which N∆ attains the worst-case critical degree. This description of the critical degree in terms of κ alone is specific to the dense regime. In the logarithmic regime, variation in the magnitude of f can shift the local nearest-neighbor angle by a constant amount. The sufficient condition for N∆ therefore depends on both the global upper bound on f and the ratio between its essential upper and lower bounds over the sphere. The resulting conditions are stated in Theorem 4.4. A different graph construction gives a stronger guarantee for sparse regions that are not covered by these conditions. If f is bounded above by eo(d), then at every fixed degree exponent strictly between β∗(c) and 1, the constructed graph achieves exact recovery from every entry for most uniform queries, even when f is arbitrarily small or zero on parts of the sphere. Its evaluation cost has the same exponential scale as its degree. In the other direction, there are densities that place sufficiently large probability mass in a small region and force every navigation graph to use a degree exponent strictly larger than β∗(c), even for uniform queries and even when the graph is designed after observing the complete sample. We prove the navigation guarantee for this construction in Theorem 4.6 and the lower bound in Theorem 4.8. 

1.3 Main results 

We now state the navigation guarantees and lower bounds associated with the curves in (2) and (3). We then quantify the cost of fresh random starts and the quality of the point returned when exact recovery is relaxed. The degree and density exponents in these statements are fixed constants. The 4words above and below refer to a fixed positive margin between the degree exponent and the relevant threshold exponent. 

Theorem 1.1 (Exact navigation above the curve, informal) . Fix k ≥ 1 and a query distribution Q

with a density on Sd−1, fixed before the data are sampled. The following conclusions hold. (a) Suppose that the data are uniform. In the logarithmic regime, let ∆ = nβ with β∗(c) < β < 1.In the dense regime, let ∆ = eγd with γ > γ c. In either regime, choose ρ so that nF d−1(ρ) = ∆ .Then, with high probability, greedy navigation on Gρ and N∆ succeeds from every entry for every query outside a set of Q-measure o(1) . The searches use O(1) greedy moves in the logarithmic regime and eo(d) moves in the dense regime, so their evaluation cost is ∆1+ o(1) .(b) In the dense regime, suppose that the data are sampled from P = f dσ d−1. Let 0 ≤ κ ≤ 

> 12

log(3 /2) and ∆ = eγd , and choose ¯κ with κ ≤ ¯κ < γ . Assume ρmin = e−o(d), and fix a sufficiently large constant depending on γ, κ, and ¯κ. Suppose that, for every x ∈ Sd−1, the ratio between the essential upper and lower bounds of f in the cap centered at x whose radius is this constant times ρ(x) is at most e¯κd . If e2γ > 4e2κ − 2 with a fixed margin, then, with high probability, greedy navigation on N∆ succeeds from every entry for every query q satisfying the density ratio bound eκd in the cap centered at q whose radius is the same constant times 

ρ(q), except for queries in a set of Q-measure o(1) . The search uses eo(d) greedy moves and has evaluation cost ∆1+ o(1) .

We next turn to the complementary behavior below the curve. For uniform data, the lower bound is measured under the uniform query distribution. For nonuniform data, the lower-bound construction distributes the query mass among a growing number of separated regions across the sphere. We call a sequence Qn globally spread if, for every fixed 0 < θ < π/ 2, sup p∈Sd−1 Pq∼Qn (q ∈ C(p, θ )) −→ 0. 

Theorem 1.2 (Failure of exact navigation below the curve, informal) . Fix k ≥ 1.(a) Suppose that the data are uniform. In the logarithmic regime, let ∆ = nβ with 0 < β < β ∗(c).In the dense regime, let ∆ = eγd with 0 < γ < γ c. Then there is a constant a > 0 such that, with high probability over the data, every G admits a measurable query set of surface measure 

1 − o(1) on which every query q has at least (1 − o(1)) na entries in the logarithmic regime and at least (1 − o(1)) ead entries in the dense regime satisfying WG(q; x0) ≥ ∆. If ∆( G) ≤ ∆,every query in this set has at least (1 − o(1)) na failing entries in the logarithmic regime and at least (1 − o(1)) ead failing entries in the dense regime. Moreover, with high probability over the data, any G that succeeds from every entry on a query set of surface measure 1 − o(1) has average degree at least (∆ ∗)1−o(1) , and every query in its success set outside a subset of surface measure o(1) has evaluation cost at least (∆ ∗)1−o(1) from a 1 − o(1) fraction of entries. (b) In the dense regime, fix κ ≥ 0 and let ∆ = eγd , where γc < γ ≤ log 2 and e2γ < 4e2κ − 2.There exist a positive sampling density f whose values across Sd−1 vary by exactly a factor 

eκd after sets of surface measure zero are ignored, and a globally spread query distribution Qn

with a density on Sd−1. The sampling density and query distribution are fixed before the data are sampled, and ρmin = e−o(d). With high probability over the data, every G of degree at most 

∆ has an exceptional query set of Qn-measure o(1) , possibly depending on G, outside which greedy navigation fails from at least eΩ( d) entries. 

Together, Theorems 1.1 and 1.2 establish the critical curve for exact navigation from every entry. For uniform data, the curve gives the sharp degree and evaluation-cost exponents. Figure 1: The uniform critical curve. For uniform data with d = ⌊c log n⌋ and degree ∆ = nβ , the solid curve is β = β∗(c). In the shaded region above it, N∆ succeeds from every entry for most uniform queries. Below it, every graph of degree at most ∆ has failing entries for most uniform queries. Both statements hold with probability 1 − o(1) over the data when β stays a fixed positive distance from β∗(c) (Theorems 1.1 and 1.2). The dotted line β = γcc approximates the critical curve for small c, since β∗(c) ∼ γcc as c → 0. and for 0 ≤ κ ≤ 12 log(3 /2), N∆ attains the optimal worst-case degree threshold in (3) among all navigation graphs, including those designed after observing the complete sample. Allowing fresh starting points offers a way to retain exact recovery at degrees below the threshold for success from every entry. A uniform random entry is a data label chosen uniformly and independently of the query and the sampled coordinates. Independent restarts repeat greedy navigation on the same sampled graph from fresh uniform random entries. To compare restarts with more flexible searches, we introduce local exploration algorithms . At each step, such an algorithm may request an entry or expand an already evaluated point whose neighbor list has not yet been scanned. Requested labels either follow a sequence fixed before sampling or are drawn afresh uniformly and independently of the sampled coordinates and the exploration history. Requesting an entry evaluates its distance to the query, and expanding a point scans its entire neighbor list. Success is recorded after the first entry request or complete expansion whose evaluated points include a member of Top k(q). The cost counts all distinct points evaluated through that operation, across all earlier entry requests and complete expansions. The decision to request an entry and the choice of a point to expand may depend on the query, the algorithm’s internal randomness, and the complete exploration history, consisting of the requested and expanded labels, scanned lists, and positions of evaluated points. All random entry choices are included in the algorithm’s randomness. Beam search belongs to this class when started from such entries because it retains several evaluated candidates and selects which to expand. 

Theorem 1.3 (The exact cost of relaxing the entry guarantee, informal) . Fix k ≥ 1. For uniform data in the dense regime, let ∆ = eγd , where 12 log 43 ≤ γ < γ c, and choose ρ so that nF d−1(ρ) = ∆ .For the explicit exponent η∗(γ) > 0 in (50) , and for every query fixed before sampling, one greedy run from a uniform random entry succeeds with probability e−(η∗(γ)+ o(1)) d over the data and the entry on both Gρ and N∆. On either graph, every local exploration algorithm requires at least e(γ+η∗(γ)−o(1)) d

distinct evaluations to achieve constant success probability. This lower bound is attained on Gρ by 

6independent restarts, which succeed with probability 1 − o(1) over the data and the restart entries using at most e(γ+η∗(γ)+ o(1)) d distinct evaluations. Optimizing the degree over this range gives the exponent min 12 log(4 /3) ≤γ<γ c {γ + η∗(γ)} ≈ 0.243 , below the every-entry exponent γc ≈ 0.347 .

The preceding theorem quantifies the saving in evaluation cost obtained by allowing fresh starts while retaining exact recovery. A run that fails to find the exact nearest neighbor, however, may still return a point close to the query. At the degrees used for fresh starts, how close to the query can greedy navigation get from every entry? We answer this question by bounding the rank of the stopping point of a full greedy run , which continues until no listed neighbor is closer. Here rank refers to the ordering of the data by distance to q, with rank 1 denoting the exact nearest neighbor. For uniform data in the dense regime, let ∆ = eγd with 0 < γ < γ c and choose ρ so that 

nF d−1(ρ) = ∆. The exponent governing the rank bound is νγ = 12 log  e4γ /(4( e2γ − 1)) . For every fixed ε > 0, with high probability every full greedy run on Gρ or N∆ from every entry stops at a point of rank at most e(νγ +ε)d for every query outside a set whose measure under σd−1 is e−Ω( d).For nonuniform data, relaxing exact recovery to a bound on rank also permits greater density variation while preserving the guarantee from every entry. Fix ε > 0 and a query distribution Q

with a density on Sd−1, chosen before the data are sampled. In the dense regime, let ∆ = eγd with 

γc < γ ≤ log 2 and 0 ≤ κ < γ − γc, and assume the regularity and minimum-radius conditions of Theorem 5.11, with density ratio at most eκd in the prescribed cap at every center. Then, with high probability, every full greedy run on N∆ from every entry stops within eo(d) moves at a point of rank at most 2 e(κ+ε)d for every query outside a set of probability o(1) under Q.

1.4 Technical overview 

The lens geometry determines whether a single greedy step can improve the current point, whereas the results in Section 1.3 concern the outcome and cost of the entire search. Turning the estimate for one step into these results creates three separate proof problems. Above the curve, we must force progress from every entry and rule out stopping near the query before the nearest neighbor is found. For nonuniform data, this argument must also account for changes in the direction distributions of closer points and in the distance covered by a neighbor list of fixed length. Below the curve, the lower bound must hold even when all neighbor lists are chosen after the complete sample is known. When fresh random entries are allowed, we must show that adaptive local exploration cannot improve on independent restarts per operation at the exponential scale. We must also show that the success probability of one run reflects the fraction of successful entries in most sampled data sets. Above the curve, we average over possible stopping points and compare directional distributions. Below the curve, we combine sampling and geometric estimates that hold for all graphs chosen from the sample. For fresh starts, we compare all adaptive routes and then resample the data to analyze two entries on the same graph. 

1.4.1 Navigation above the curve 

We begin with uniform data and k = 1, since finding the nearest neighbor also gives the result for every larger fixed k. Near the nearest-neighbor scale, only a constant number of closer points are expected. An empty improving lens can therefore mean either that navigation has failed or that the current point is already the nearest neighbor. Choose an auxiliary neighbor radius r for which 

nF d−1(r) lies above the critical degree and below ∆ by fixed exponential margins. We distinguish the two cases through false local minima, points that have a closer data point but no closer neighbor in Gr. For a data point x at distance θ from q, let t = Fd−1(θ) be the surface mass of closer locations 7and let ℓ = σd−1(C(x, r ) ∩ C(q, θ )) be the mass of the improving lens. Independence gives 

P{x is a false local minimum | x} = (1 − ℓ)n−1 − (1 − t)n−1.

The difference of powers is crucial because it remains small after summing over all possible stopping points. We choose a final query cap slightly larger than C(q, θ 1) and small enough relative to r that Theorem 2.4(a) gives ℓ ≥ (1 − e−Ω( d))t throughout it. The mean value theorem bounds the difference of powers by ( n − 1)( t − ℓ)(1 − ℓ)n−2. For uniform x and fixed q, the variable t is uniform on [0 , 1]. Using t − ℓ ≤ e−Ω( d)t together with this lower bound on ℓ, summing over the n

possible choices of x leaves an exponentially small factor times the cancellation 

n(n − 1) 

Z 10

t(1 − t)n−2 dt = 1 .

The expected number of false local minima in the final cap is therefore exponentially small. Averaging over queries and applying Markov’s inequality shows that, with probability 1 − o(1) over the sample, every run entering the cap finds the nearest neighbor for most queries. This conclusion inside the final cap gives an every-entry guarantee only after we show that every run starting outside the cap reaches it. At the cap boundary, almost all of a slightly smaller query cap lies within distance r of the current point and gives a prescribed decrease in query distance. This smaller cap contains exponentially many data points in expectation, so the probability that it contains no such neighbor is doubly exponentially small. For a current point farther from q, the set of locations giving the same decrease within distance r has at least as much surface measure. Hence every move outside the final cap decreases the distance to q by a fixed fraction of θ1, and the run enters the cap after O(1 /θ 1) moves. It remains to control the additional moves inside the final cap, where each improvement may be much smaller. Any two visited points separated by at least two moves must be more than r

apart, since otherwise the later point would have been a better successor at the earlier point. The points in the even and odd positions therefore form two sets whose distinct members are more than 

r apart. Summing the pairwise squared distances within either set and using that all its points lie in the final cap bounds its size by a constant independent of d. Together with the moves before entering the final cap, this gives O(1) moves in the logarithmic regime and eo(d) moves in the dense regime. Finally, concentration and the exponential gap between nF d−1(r) and ∆ show that Gρ and 

N∆ contain every edge of Gr and have neighbor lists of length O(∆). Since each expansion scans a complete neighbor list, the evaluation cost is at most ∆ 1+ o(1) .The same separation between reaching the final cap and completing the search inside it applies to nonuniform data in the dense regime, but density variation changes both the mass of an improving lens and the distance covered by a nearest-neighbor list. Inside the final cap, we choose one auxiliary radius no larger than the distance from any data point in that cap to the farthest point in its list, so every such list contains all data points within this radius. Fixing the current point’s distance from 

q, we represent the current point and a point drawn from P , conditional on being closer to q, by the directions from q toward them, represented by their normalized projections onto the subspace orthogonal to q. Replacing the closer direction by its opposite turns failure to lie in the auxiliary neighbor cap into a lower bound on the inner product of these two directions. The density bound near q with parameter κ controls both direction densities relative to uniform measure. The false local minimum probability is a convex nondecreasing function of the closer P -mass outside the neighbor cap, so we can maximize its average by concentrating the two direction densities on caps with a common axis (see Theorem 4.2). This replaces arbitrary direction densities by an explicit configuration whose exponential rate can be calculated. 8Keeping this directional comparison sharp requires averaging over the current point’s distance before applying an upper bound to its direction density. At a fixed distance from q, the direction density is normalized by the mean of f over all directions at that distance. The density of the current point’s distance contains the same mean, so the two factors cancel upon integration. The remaining cap constraints require the final query cap to contain exponentially many data points at the density lower bound and the auxiliary neighbor cap to remain within the degree budget at the density upper bound. Cap measures scale with the ( d − 1)st power of the sine of the radius. To identify the critical degree, we examine these constraints as the fixed auxiliary margins shrink. Near the boundary, the ratio of the sine of the final query cap radius to the sine of the auxiliary neighbor radius approaches eκ−γ from above, while the exponent in the upper bound on the expected number of possible stopping points approaches κ.The rearranged configuration then determines the critical curve. After replacing both direction densities by caps with a common axis, we split each cap according to projection onto that axis. The projection threshold for a closer point is chosen so that the uniform probability beyond it offsets the factor eκd from the density bound. The threshold for the current point similarly offsets the upper bound on the expected number of possible stopping points. In the limiting comparison, the smallest product of thresholds that meets both requirements approaches 1 − e−2κ. The spherical cosine identity gives the inner product required for the closer point to miss the auxiliary neighbor cap, which approaches e2( γ−κ)/2 − 1. When this value exceeds the product of the two projection thresholds, the normalized components orthogonal to the common axis must have inner product bounded below by a positive constant, an event with exponentially small probability. The required inequality is 

e2( γ−κ)

2 − 1 > 1 − e−2κ ⇐⇒ e2γ > 4e2κ − 2.

Under this strict inequality, the directional comparison and the averaging over the current point’s distance make the expected number of false local minima exponentially small. To obtain a guarantee from every entry, we must also force runs starting outside the final cap to reach it. Just outside the cap, the density bound with parameter κ provides exponentially many candidate neighbors that reduce the query distance by a fixed factor. At points x farther from q, the density bound with parameter ¯ κ < γ at every center provides exponentially many candidate neighbors that reduce the distance by at least a constant times ρf (x). Combining this progress toward the cap with 

ρf (x) ≥ ρmin = e−o(d) and the bound on moves inside the cap gives eo(d) moves from every entry and evaluation cost ∆ 1+ o(1) .The above dense regime argument uses the fact that the nearest-neighbor angle tends to zero. In the logarithmic regime, this angle remains bounded away from zero, so the sufficient condition for N∆ involves both the density level and a density ratio bound requiring a positive lower bound. To allow arbitrarily low or zero density, we use a different graph construction. When f ≤ eo(d),the first task is to show that most query caps have enough P -mass even when the density at their centers is arbitrarily small or zero. We reduce the cap radius slightly and average the masses of caps of the reduced radius over their centers. Since no cap has more than eo(d) times its uniform mass, the centers whose reduced caps have at least half their uniform mass form a set of surface measure e−o(d). Spherical isoperimetry shows that enlarging this set by any fixed positive angle covers almost the whole sphere. For most queries, a reduced cap about one of these centers lies inside the original query cap, giving the required lower bound on its P -mass at an arbitrarily small cost in the exponent. The resulting lower bound on the P -mass of most query caps supplies the sampled points needed by the graph construction. We use a fixed sequence of query caps shrinking toward the nearest-neighbor scale and subsamples whose sizes increase as the caps shrink. Every neighbor list 9contains a common small subsample, which lets distant entries reach the largest query cap, together with nearby points from the larger subsamples. From a point in one query cap, the intersection of its neighbor cap with the next smaller query cap has enough P -mass to contain a point from the larger subsample assigned to that next query cap, except with doubly exponentially small probability. Increasing the subsample sizes compensates for the shrinking query caps, while the density upper bound keeps the neighbor lists within the degree budget. At the final scale, using the full sample in the neighbor lists supplies the remaining closer points, and the false local minimum estimate prevents the run from stopping before it reaches the nearest neighbor. The resulting graph gives exact recovery from every entry for most uniform queries whenever β∗(c) < β < 1, using O(1) moves and evaluation cost O(∆). 

1.4.2 Failure below the curve 

To show that the degree curve is sharp, the lower bound must cover graphs whose neighbor lists are chosen after the complete sample is observed. Such a graph can combine nearby edges, which exploit local clusters in the sample, with farther edges chosen to help particular query directions. We separate the stored neighbors according to their distance from the current point. Random sampling bounds the number of nearby points that improve the query distance, while spherical geometry bounds the fraction of query directions for which any fixed farther point is improving. The second estimate holds for every potential stored edge, so the two bounds can be made simultaneous for all graphs constructed from the sample. For uniform data, we select points whose ranks lie between an exponentially growing threshold above k and twice that threshold. These points provide many possible entries outside Top k(q). We split potential neighbors at a distance below the critical radius, calling points within this distance nearby and the remaining points farther. A nearby improving point must lie in a lens whose surface measure is exponentially smaller than the cap of points closer to q. Since a selected point has rank on the order of the threshold, only the same order of data points lie in this closer cap. Choosing the threshold so that its growth rate is smaller than the exponential loss of the lens therefore makes nearby improving points unlikely. Each farther point improves the distance for only an exponentially small fraction of query directions. Summing this directional bound over the allowed number of stored neighbors controls every possible neighbor list. Averaging both estimates over uniform queries shows that, with probability 1 − o(1) over the sample, every graph whose degree is below the critical scale has the following property. For most queries, only an exponentially small fraction of the selected points have an improving listed neighbor. All remaining selected points are failing entries. Averaging over uniform queries also converts the estimate for the fraction of selected points with an improving neighbor into lower bounds on average degree and evaluation cost. As a uniform query varies, every sample point belongs to the selected rank interval for nearly the same surface measure of queries. If a graph succeeds from every entry for most queries, all but a vanishing fraction of its points must therefore have neighbor lists at the critical scale. Otherwise, too many selected entries would have short lists containing no improving neighbor. Since expanding an entry scans its complete neighbor list, starting from a point with such a long list already incurs the corresponding evaluation cost. For an arbitrary graph, the same argument uses a tested degree ∆ below the critical scale. For most uniform queries, all but a vanishing fraction of the selected entries either have no improving listed neighbor and fail or evaluate more than ∆ points in their first expansion. These alternatives give WG(q; x0) ≥ ∆ for the selected entries in Theorem 1.2(a). The nonuniform lower bound also separates nearby improving edges from farther ones, but matching the shifted curve requires a sampling density that suppresses both kinds of improvement. We place density close to 1 in an inner ball and outside a surrounding annulus, while lowering the 10 annulus density by a factor eκd . The inner ball supplies many entries, and the exterior supplies the points in Top k(q). We place the queries at a prescribed distance from the common center and select inner points within a prescribed distance of the query. These selected entries and closer exterior points lie on opposite sides of the query, as in the rearranged configuration of the upper bound. The geometry is chosen so that, at degree eγd , farther exterior points improve selected entries for only an exponentially small fraction of the query locations, even after summing over all selected entries and their neighbor lists. If the annulus instead had density close to 1, the expected number of points there that are closer to q than a selected entry would have exponent approaching  

> 12

log(( e2γ + 2) /4) (see (23)). Lowering the annulus density by eκd makes such an intermediate point exponentially unlikely when κ exceeds 12 log(( e2γ + 2) /4) by a fixed amount. Random sampling also rules out nearby exterior points that improve the query distance. For all remaining query locations, every improving move from a selected inner point remains among the selected inner points, and no complete neighbor list scanned there contains a point of Top k(q). Placing separated copies of this construction across the sphere gives the globally spread query distribution in Theorem 1.2(b). The logarithmic regime requires a different obstruction because the nearest-neighbor angle stays bounded away from zero. We therefore concentrate most of the sampling mass in a cap. For most uniform queries, Top k(q) lies inside the cap while many possible entries lie outside it. Success from every entry would require the graph to provide improving edges from exterior points into the cap for most query directions. Random sampling rules out nearby improving edges. For farther edges, the directional estimate gives a probability that is exponentially smaller than one over the product of the degree and the number of exterior points. Summing over all exterior points and their at most ∆ stored neighbors therefore still leaves only an exponentially small fraction of queries with an improving edge into the cap. For every graph of degree at most ∆, most queries consequently have failing exterior entries. Concentrating most of the sampling mass in a cap of exponentially small surface measure raises the necessary degree exponent above the uniform value β∗(c) (see Theorem 4.8). 

1.4.3 Relaxing the navigation guarantee 

For uniform data, failure from many entries below the critical degree leaves open how many fresh starts exact recovery requires. The difficulty is to compare a direct jump toward the nearest neighbor with routes through arbitrarily many intermediate points. A successful search must cross a range of query distances where improving neighbors are exponentially rare. Write θtrap for its outer boundary, where their expected number has exponent zero. Let gρ(φ → ψ) be the exponent governing the expected number of neighbors in Gρ near query distance ψ from a point at distance 

φ. This combines an edge probability, symmetric in its endpoints, with the number of candidate points at the destination. With χρ(φ, ψ ) = 12 (gρ(φ → ψ) + gρ(ψ → φ)), the rate separates into a symmetric term and a logarithmic correction as 

gρ(φ → ψ) = χρ(φ, ψ ) + 12 log sin ψ

sin φ .

The logarithmic terms telescope along a route. In the degree range of Theorem 1.3, the symmetric term is largest on the diagonal for a fixed mean query distance, negative inside the trap boundary, and increasing in each distance. Intermediate steps can therefore only lower the total exponent. The best route jumps directly from the outer boundary to the nearest-neighbor scale, with exponent 

−η∗(γ). To turn this route comparison into a lower bound for adaptive exploration, we must account for choices made after earlier neighbor lists have been read. Revealing a cap containing each scanned 11 list leaves the unrevealed points independent and uniform outside the exposed regions. With at most eO(d) entry requests and expansions, these regions have total measure o(1) in the dense regime. Dividing query distances into short intervals then lets us sum over all possible routes without fixing the algorithm’s choices in advance. Fresh entries hit C(q, θ trap ) with probability e−ω(d), so constant success probability requires e(η∗(γ)−o(1)) d expansions. Caching creates a further difficulty, since many expansions could evaluate the same points. Every set of expanded points of size below eη∗(γ)d by a fixed exponent margin contains a subset of at least a 1 /d fraction whose points are separated by a fixed fraction of θ1. The total pairwise overlap of these neighbor lists is exponentially smaller than the sum of their lengths. The union therefore has at least the degree times the number of expansions, up to a subexponential factor, giving the distinct-evaluation exponent γ + η∗(γ). To attain the preceding evaluation cost lower bound by independent restarts, we show that, for each query fixed before sampling and most sampled threshold graphs, greedy search succeeds from a fraction at least e−(η∗(γ)+ o(1)) d of entries. With the nearest neighbor removed and its distance fixed, the greedy path on the remaining points is independent of the nearest neighbor’s hidden direction. The probability that this direction places the nearest neighbor in a neighbor cap along the path gives the single-run exponent. An estimate up to eo(d) factors, however, cannot establish concentration of the successful-entry fraction by a direct second-moment calculation. We retain paths of subexponential length that stay outside a cap slightly smaller than C(q, θ trap ), preserving the single-run exponent. Resampling after revealing one such path produces a second path independent of the first, and rotational symmetry makes their joint success probability against the hidden direction an exact product. We then compare the second path on the original and resampled data. For the retained paths, the probability that replaced points change the second path has decay exponent strictly larger than η∗(γ). To affect joint success, such a change must also occur with a hidden direction that makes the first path succeed, contributing a further factor e−(η∗(γ)−o(1)) d. The resulting error is smaller than the product of the success probabilities, so the fraction of successful entries concentrates. Independent restarts on the same graph therefore attain the matching exponent. Minimizing γ + η∗(γ) over the degree range in Theorem 1.3 gives approximately 0 .243. Even when some entries do not reach the nearest neighbor, the subcritical lens estimates force every full greedy run to stop inside a specified query cap. The number of sampled points in that cap therefore bounds the stopping rank. For nonuniform data, the progress estimates outside the final cap in the upper-bound proof remain valid over a wider density range than exact recovery, yielding the rank guarantee (see Theorem 5.11). 

1.5 Related work 

Random input models have enabled quantitative analyses of greedy search on specific proximity graphs. Laarhoven [Laa18] derived tradeoffs between query time and space for greedy walks with random restarts, matching the hashing and filtering tradeoff of [ALRW17] as the approximation factor approaches one and storage approaches linear. These methods build on locality-sensitive hashing and filtering [AR15, BDGL16]. For uniform spherical data, Prokhorenkova and Shekhovtsov [PS20] proved exact navigation from the prescribed random entry for log log n ≪ d ≪ log n when the query is sampled near a designated nearest neighbor and the neighbor radius exceeds √2 times the nearest-neighbor scale. With beam search and added long edges, their query time exponent, normalized by dimension using natural logarithms, can approach 12 log(27 /16) ≈ 0.262 [PS20, Corollary 3]. Subsequent analyses address missing edges [SSX23], the degree and expected path length of sparse neighborhood graphs [MZZ +26], and the transition from power-law to subpolynomial growth in search cost as the data set grows relative to its intrinsic dimension [MED26]. Random input analyses also cover hierarchical graphs on a torus, including models with densities bounded above and below 12 by fixed positive constants [BDSS26]. For d = o(log n/ log log n), that hierarchical graph analysis gives approximate recovery from every entry for all queries and expected move bounds for a fixed query. Alongside analyses of prescribed graphs, navigable graph theory asks how many edges are needed to provide an improving neighbor from every data point toward every other data point. [DGM +24] gave sparse constructions for arbitrary point sets and average-degree lower bounds for random points in O(log n) dimensions. Khanna, Padaki, and Waingarten [KPW26] related sparse graph construction to set cover and gave approximation algorithms and hardness results, while [CDFC +26] developed faster construction algorithms. Allowing each point a small fraction of destinations for which no improving edge is required permits sparser graphs [AM26]. Waizer and Danieli [WD26] proved matching sparsity lower bounds, including a lower bound on every vertex’s degree for random spherical data, by bounding how many ordered pairs of data points a stored edge can serve. Adaptive beam search connects these guarantees for destinations in the data set to external queries, giving approximate recovery and exact recovery for a particular parameter choice [AJDG +25]. External queries can also be handled by imposing geometric conditions on an otherwise arbitrary data set. Indyk and Xu [IX23] proved approximate search guarantees for a variant of DiskANN with slower preprocessing on data of bounded intrinsic dimension. Its query cost is polylogarithmic in the ratio of the largest to the smallest pairwise distance. They also constructed inputs on which the published HNSW, NSG, and faster preprocessing DiskANN implementations empirically take linear query time to achieve reasonable accuracy. [GKSW25] strengthened the DiskANN guarantees and analyzed beam search. Sparse graphs for approximate search have also been constructed in doubling metrics and Euclidean space [AM93, LT25, HPRR26]. Using a sparse graph together with an auxiliary data structure, [GHPSV26] obtained logarithmic query time in the data set size for fixed dimension and a fixed approximation factor. Pruning constructions give exact recovery when the query lies within a prescribed distance of the data [HD16, PCC +23, LYL26]. Even on a fixed graph, the entry rule changes both the probability of success and the cost of search. Adaptive entry selection has been compared with fixed central entries through theoretical bounds and experiments [OM24]. Laarhoven [Laa18, full version, Appendix A.3] analyzed random restarts. For restarts on one sampled graph, Prokhorenkova and Shekhovtsov [PS20, Supplementary Section E] identified dependence between attempts as a technical obstacle. Since caching charges repeated evaluations only once, the total cost also depends on overlap between scanned neighbor lists. [WWC +24] studied graph search hardness using the size of the union of scanned neighbor lists on a given graph. The analyses of prescribed proximity graphs establish when those constructions succeed, while our degree comparison asks whether another graph chosen after observing the sample could use a smaller degree for success from every entry. The navigable graph lower bounds already allow arbitrary graph choices, but concern destinations in the data set rather than exact nearest neighbors of external queries. For nonuniform data, the hierarchical graph analysis uses fixed density bounds, whereas we allow density ratios exponential in dimension and determine their effect on the required degree in the dense regime over the variation range in (3). For restarts, we address the dependence between attempts identified by Prokhorenkova and Shekhovtsov and compare independent restarts with local exploration algorithms that cache evaluated points, in the graph models and degree range of Theorem 1.3. 13 2 Geometric foundations of the critical curve 

In this section, we develop geometric and probability estimates that connect neighbor radii, improving steps, and ranks under uniform and nonuniform sampling. 

2.1 Conventions 

For a query q and x ∈ Dn, define rank q(x) = 1 + # {y ∈ Dn : δ(q, y ) < δ (q, x )}. We exclude queries on the bisectors {q ∈ Sd−1 : δ(q, x ) = δ(q, y )} of distinct data points x, y ∈ Dn, since each bisector has surface measure zero. For a query fixed before sampling, distance ties also have data probability zero. For a nonempty neighbor list G(x), the successor of x is its listed point closest to q. Write 

x0, x 1, . . . for the successive points visited by a greedy run, whose distances to q strictly decrease. A run that succeeds within H complete expansions has evaluation cost at most 1 + H∆( G), including the entry and the entire final expansion. For the asymptotic bounds, we assume throughout that d → ∞ , d = O(log n), and log n = o(d2). Unless stated otherwise, the constants implicit in O(·) and Ω( ·) may depend on k, on c in the logarithmic regime, and on the fixed exponents, multipliers, and margins in the hypotheses, including 

β, γ, κ and ε where present. All bounds hold for sufficiently large n, with the threshold allowed to depend on the sequence d = d(n).

The lens estimate varies the neighbor for a fixed query, whereas a bound for one stored edge must fix its endpoints x, y and vary the query direction. The next lemma estimates the fraction of these directions for which y is closer to the query than x.16 Lemma 2.3 (Improving directions) . Let x, y ∈ Sd−1 with δ(x, y ) = t ∈ (0 , π ), and let 0 < θ < π/ 2.Among the queries q satisfying δ(q, x ) = θ, those for which δ(q, y ) < θ form a spherical cap in the (d − 2) -sphere of directions at x. Its cosine threshold is h = cot θ tan( t/ 2) . The cap is empty when h ≥ 1. When h < 1, its normalized measure is at most √d − 1 (1 − h2)(d−4) /2

q · y > cos θ gives

Thus the improving directions form a spherical cap with cosine threshold h. Because tan( t/ 2) increases with t, the threshold increases and the cap measure decreases as t increases. When h ≥ 1, no unit vector v can satisfy v · w > h , so the cap is empty. It remains to estimate the measure of this cap. The geometric estimates give cap probabilities, while navigation depends on the points present in the sample. To relate these probabilities to rank, suppose that the data are uniform, fix a label i

2.5 Nonuniform local scales 

Under nonuniform sampling, the number of points in a cap is governed by its P -probability rather than its surface measure. To use the geometric estimates in this setting, we control the density on neighborhoods scaled by the local radii from Section 1.2. We retain ρf (x) = ρ(x) for the smallest radius whose cap has P -probability at least 1 /n , so nP (C(x, ρ f (x))) = 1 and ρmin = inf x∈Sd−1 ρf (x). For degree ∆ = ⌈eγd ⌉ with fixed γ > 0 in the dense regime, (6) gives neighbor radius ( eγ + o(1)) θ1

under uniform sampling. We therefore control the nonuniform density on C(x, Ke γ ρf (x)), where the fixed constant K > 0 specifies the size of the neighborhood. For κ ≥ 0, we call f (κ, K )-regular 

if log(1 /ρ min ) = o(d) and, at every center x, the essential oscillation of log f on C(x, Ke γ ρf (x)) is at most κd . For a measurable set A, this means ess osc A log f = ess sup A log f − ess inf A log f . The density thus varies by at most a factor eκd on each such cap. The condition on ρmin keeps the later expansion bound O(1 + ρ−1min ) subexponential in d. Uniform data satisfy these conditions with κ = 0 and ρf (x) = θ1.The density bound transfers the surface measures in the lens estimates to probabilities under P .If measurable sets A and B of positive surface measure lie in one cap C(x, Ke γ ρf (x)), integrating 18 the upper and lower density bounds over them gives

λ + μ changes both the local nearest-neighbor radius and the radius reached by a neighbor list of prescribed length. The logarithmic nonuniform bounds in Sections 4.4 and 4.5 therefore depend on density level and variation. 

# 3 The uniform critical curve 

We now prove that (7) gives the critical degree scale for uniform data. The upper bound applies to graphs that retain every edge of a threshold graph. We say that G contains Gr if Gr(x) ⊆ G(x) for every x ∈ Dn. The following theorem states the matching upper and lower bounds. 

Theorem 3.1 (Uniform critical degree) . For uniform data on Sd−1, fix k ≥ 1. In the logarithmic regime, let ∆ = ⌈nβ ⌉ with fixed 0 < β < 1. In the dense regime, let ∆ = ⌈eγd ⌉ with fixed γ > 0.

(a) (Above the critical degree.) Fix an absolutely continuous query distribution Q before drawing the data. In the logarithmic regime, suppose that β > β ∗(c), choose β′ ∈ (β∗(c), β ), and set 

ρ′ = ρβ′ . There is a constant η > 0 such that, with probability at least 1 − n−η over Dn, there is a measurable set QD ⊆ Sd−1 with Pq∼Q(q ∈ QD) ≥ 1 − n−η on which every graph G containing 

Gρ′ and satisfying ∆( G) ≤ 2∆ succeeds from every entry. Each search uses O(1) moves and has evaluation cost at most ∆1+ o(1) . In the dense regime, suppose that γ > γ c, choose γ′ ∈ (γc, γ ),and set ρ′ = rγ′ . There are positive constants c1 and c2 such that, with probability at least 

1 − e−c1d over Dn, there is a measurable set QD ⊆ Sd−1 with Pq∼Q(q ∈ QD) ≥ 1 − e−c2d on which every graph G containing Gρ′ and satisfying ∆( G) ≤ 2∆ succeeds from every entry. Each search uses O(1 /θ 1) = eo(d) moves and has evaluation cost at most ∆1+ o(1) . These data events can be chosen so that the same query set, success guarantee, move bound, and evaluation cost bound also apply to Gρβ in the logarithmic regime, Grγ in the dense regime, and N∆ in both regimes. 

(b) (Below the critical degree.) In the logarithmic regime, suppose that β < β ∗(c). There are positive constants a and η such that, with probability at least 1 − n−η over Dn, every navigation graph 

G constructed from the sample admits a measurable set QG ⊆ Sd−1 with σd−1(QG) ≥ 1 − n−η

such that, for every q ∈ QG, at least (1 − o(1)) na entries x0 satisfy WG(q; x0) ≥ ∆. In the dense regime, suppose that γ < γ c. There are positive constants a, c1, and c2 such that, with probability at least 1 − e−c1d over Dn, every navigation graph G constructed from the sample admits a measurable set QG ⊆ Sd−1 with σd−1(QG) ≥ 1 − e−c2d such that, for every q ∈ QG,at least (1 − o(1)) ead entries x0 satisfy WG(q; x0) ≥ ∆. If ∆( G) ≤ ∆, then for every q ∈ QG,greedy navigation fails from at least (1 − o(1)) na entries in the logarithmic regime and at least 

(1 − o(1)) ead entries in the dense regime. 

19 To prove part (a), we first find conditions on a general radius r that force progress on every graph containing Gr. We then choose r = ρ′ in Section 3.2 and check that the threshold and nearest-neighbor graphs retain these edges within the required degree bound. 

3.1 Uniform progress from short edges 

Short edges must force progress toward a cap around the query and prevent unsuccessful stopping inside that cap. Fix r ∈ (0 , π/ 2) and choose the cap radius A so that θ1 < A < π/ 2 and cos 2 A > cos r.To prevent unsuccessful stopping inside C(q, A ), we need almost all locations closer to q than the current point to lie within its neighbor radius r. By Theorem 2.4(a), the fraction of closer locations beyond radius r is exponentially small whenever τ = (cos 2 A − cos r)/ sin 2 A has a fixed positive lower bound. The inequality cos 2 A > cos r will also bound the number of visited points inside the cap. For progress outside the cap, put h = ( A − θ1)/2. We will require each move there to decrease the distance to q by at least h. From a point at distance A from q, every point of C(q, A − h) gives this decrease, and A − h > θ 1. Write cpop = d−1 log( nF (A − h)) for the exponent of its expected number of data points. A fixed positive lower bound on cpop makes this expectation exponential in 

d. We choose r and A in Section 3.2 so that τ and cpop are bounded below by positive constants. For a fixed query q, let P(q) be the event that every data point x outside C(q, A ) has a data point y with δ(x, y ) ≤ r and δ(q, y ) ≤ δ(q, x ) − h. On any graph containing Gr, this forces a decrease of at least h whenever an expansion outside the cap does not already succeed. After entering the cap, a run stays there because its distances decrease. To exclude unsuccessful stopping inside it, we consider false local minima relative to Top k(q), namely data points outside Top k(q) with no closer neighbor in Gr. Let C(q) be the event that C(q, A ) contains no such point. The next proposition establishes both properties for most queries on one sample and bounds the search from every entry.

θ1 < A < π/ 2 and cos 2 A > cos r. Assume that τ ≥ τ0 for a fixed τ0 > 0 and that cpop is bounded below by a positive constant independent of n. Fix an absolutely continuous query distribution Q

before sampling. For each sample Dn, let QD ⊆ Sd−1 be the measurable set of queries for which both

is nonnegative. Increasing θ therefore lowers the threshold on Z, while rotational invariance leaves its distribution unchanged. The fraction of allowed directions cannot decrease at any fixed ψ. To control the measure of queries outside QD on one sample, we integrate the failure probabilities against the prescribed distribution Q. Membership in QD is measurable jointly in the data and query because C(q) and

This proves the required measure bound for QD.Fix a sample, a query q ∈ QD, and a graph G containing Gr. While a run remains outside 

C(q, A ), P(q) ensures that every expansion either succeeds or is followed by a decrease of at least 21 h. Since the entry is at distance at most π from q, there are at most ⌈(π − A)/h ⌉ + 1 expansions outside the cap. This includes a possible successful final expansion with no following move. After a run enters C(q, A ), its decreasing distances keep it there and C(q) prevents unsuccessful stopping. Inside the cap, individual improvements may be small, but any two points visited at least two moves apart have distance greater than r. Otherwise, the later point would have been a listed neighbor closer to q than the successor chosen at the earlier point. The points expanded inside the cap at even and odd positions along the run therefore form two subsequences with pairwise distances greater than r. Write either subsequence as z1, . . . , z N . Their pairwise squared Euclidean distances exceed 2(1 − cos r), whereas q · zi ≥ cos A places all of them in the Euclidean ball of radius sin A

centered at cos A q . Here cos 2 A > cos r gives 1 − cos r > sin 2 A, so the pairwise squared distances exceed twice the squared ball radius. For N ≥ 2, let ¯ z = N −1 P

C(q, A ). Adding the expansions outside C(q, A ) gives at most H complete expansions in total. Each expansion evaluates at most ∆( G) new points. Including the entry, the evaluation cost is at most 1 + H∆( G), with the successful final expansion counted in full. 

3.2 Completing the uniform upper bound 

To prove Theorem 3.1(a), we choose r and A using the gap above the critical degree. We verify the hypotheses of Theorem 3.2 in each regime, then check that the threshold and nearest-neighbor graphs contain Gr and have degree at most 2∆. 

Proof of Theorem 3.1(a). In the logarithmic regime, take r = ρβ′ with β∗(c) < β ′ < β < 1. The estimate for θ1 after Theorem 2.1, together with (6), gives θ1 → θ∞ = arcsin( e−1/c ) and 

r → r∞ = arcsin( e−(1 −β′)/c ). The inequality β′ > β ∗(c) gives cos r∞ < cos 2 θ∞, leaving room to choose a fixed A ∈ (θ∞, π/ 2) with cos 2 A > cos r∞. For all sufficiently large n, this choice gives 

θ1 < A < π/ 2 and cos 2 A > cos r, as required by Theorem 3.2. The definitions of τ and cpop , together with (5), give τ → (cos 2 A − cos r∞)/ sin 2 A > 0 and cpop → log(sin(( A + θ∞)/2) / sin θ∞) > 0, so Theorem 3.2 applies. Since h → (A − θ∞)/2 > 0 and K has a finite limit, it gives H = O(1) complete expansions. The probability of failure over the data and the excluded query probability are both e−Ω( d) = n−Ω(1) .In the dense regime, take r = rγ′ with γc < γ ′ < γ . Now (6) gives r/θ 1 → eγ′

> √2, so we can choose the cap radius as A = a′r, where a′ is a fixed constant satisfying e−γ′

< a ′ < 1/√2. The bound a′ > e −γ′

ensures A > θ 1, while a′ < 1/√2 ensures cos 2 A > cos r, for all sufficiently large n. To verify the remaining hypotheses of Theorem 3.2 and bound K, we use (5) and expand sine and cosine at zero. This gives τ → 1/(2 a′2) − 1 > 0 and cpop → log((1 + a′eγ′

)/2) > 0, while 

K → 1/(1 − 2a′2). Since h = ( 12 (a′eγ′

− 1) + o(1)) θ1, the proposition gives H = O(1 /θ 1) = eo(d)

complete expansions. The probability of failure over the data and the excluded query probability are both e−Ω( d). In either regime, the move bound is H, and every graph containing Gr with degree at most 2∆ has evaluation cost at most 1 + 2 H∆ = ∆ 1+ o(1) on the query set of Theorem 3.2. It remains to verify the required edge inclusion and degree bound for the threshold and nearest-neighbor graphs. Write s = ρβ in the logarithmic regime and s = rγ in the dense regime, so s

22 is the radius of the threshold graph in Theorem 3.1(a). Conditional on the position of a data point x, the lengths |Gs(x)| and |Gr(x)| are binomial with respective means ( n − 1) F (s) and (n − 1) F (r). Applying (9) at the respective thresholds 2 nF (s) and 2 nF (r) and taking a union bound over the n labels shows that, with probability at least 1 − 2n exp( −3nF (r)/8), every x ∈ Dn

satisfies |Gs(x)| ≤ 2nF (s) and |Gr(x)| ≤ 2nF (r). The strict gap between the degree exponents gives nF (r)/∆ → 0, so |Gr(x)| < ∆ for every x. Thus Gs contains Gr and has degree at most 2∆, and the ∆ nearest neighbors of every point include its entire list in Gr. Hence N∆ also contains Gr

and has degree ∆. Adding 2 n exp( −3nF (r)/8) to the data failure bound in Theorem 3.2 preserves the error rates stated in Theorem 3.1(a). 

3.3 Traps for arbitrary navigation graphs 

The upper bound uses short edges to force an improving move. For the lower bound, we show that a list shorter than the critical degree cannot supply such a move at many points, regardless of how the graph is chosen after observing the data. We examine points with ranks from T through 2T − 1, where T > k is an integer, and call the interval [ T, 2T ) the rank window . A cutoff radius ρ

separates possible neighbors into two groups. An improving point within distance ρ of the current point x is unlikely because the lens C(x, ρ ) ∩ C(q, δ (q, x )) has small mass. A point farther than ρ

from x improves the distance for only a small fraction of query directions. Bounding these groups separately lets us control every data-dependent neighbor list through a property of the data alone. Let L be a positive integer. We seek entries that either fail or require at least L evaluations, and call them traps. It suffices first to study lists of length at most L, since a longer list already costs more than L at the first complete expansion. To locate the chosen ranks geometrically, define 

ϑ by nF (ϑ) = T , so a cap of radius ϑ contains T data points in expectation. For 0 < θ < π/ 2with cot θ tan( ρ/ 2) < 1, write Iθ(ρ) = − 12 log(1 − cot 2 θ tan 2(ρ/ 2)). By Theorem 2.3, a fixed point at distance at least ρ from x is closer to the query than x for at most an e−dI θ (ρ)+ O(log d) fraction of the directions with δ(q, x ) = θ, provided cot θ tan( ρ/ 2) stays a fixed distance below one. Write 

I = Iϑ(ρ) and J = Jϑ(ρ), where Jθ is the lens rate from Theorem 2.2. The factor e−dJ +o(d) bounds the lens mass relative to C(q, ϑ ) when δ(q, x ) = ϑ. To obtain exponentially many entries and keep the rank fluctuations negligible after summing over labels, we require T to grow exponentially in d.The additional requirements that T e −dJ and Le −dI be exponentially small control nearby improving points and farther stored neighbors, respectively. For a fixed ctrap > 0, we assume that T ≥ ectrap d,

dJ ≥ log T + ctrap d, and dI ≥ log L + ctrap d. To keep the geometric estimates uniform, assume also that ϑ ≤ π/ 2 − ε, εϑ ≤ ρ < r ∗(ϑ), and cot ϑ tan( ρ/ 2) ≤ 1 − ε for a fixed ε > 0. Here r∗(ϑ) is the lens boundary defined by cos r∗(ϑ) = cos 2 ϑ in Section 2. We first prove the lower bound under these conditions and then choose T , L, and ρ below the critical degree in each regime. 

Theorem 3.3 (Traps in a rank window) . Let T > k and L be positive integers, let ρ ∈ (0 , π/ 2) be the distance cutoff, and retain ϑ, I = Iϑ(ρ), and J = Jϑ(ρ) as defined above. Suppose that T ≥ ectrap d,

dJ ≥ log T + ctrap d, and dI ≥ log L + ctrap d. Assume also that ϑ ≤ π/ 2 − ε, εϑ ≤ ρ < r ∗(ϑ), and 

cot ϑ tan( ρ/ 2) ≤ 1 − ε, for fixed ctrap , ε > 0. For all sufficiently large n, with probability at least 

1 − e−ctrap d/ 4 over the uniform data set Dn, simultaneously for every navigation graph G constructed from the data, there is a measurable query set QG with σd−1(QG) ≥ 1 − e−ctrap d/ 8 such that, for every 

q ∈ QG, at least (1 − e−ctrap d/ 8)T entries x0 of rank in [T, 2T ) satisfy WG(q; x0) ≥ L. The same entries can be chosen so that each either has dG(x0) > L or is a failing entry with WG(q; x0) = ∞.Proof. We first consider a graph H of degree at most L and bound how many points in the rank window have a listed neighbor closer to the query. We separately bound points with rank in [ T, 2T ) but px(q) /∈ W , nearby improving points with px(q) ∈ W , and farther listed neighbors. The first two bounds use the randomness of the data. The farther-neighbor bound holds for every fixed data set and every list of length at most L.To apply the rates J and I throughout W, we first compare the corresponding angles with ϑ. If

If instead dG(x0) > L ,its first complete expansion evaluates all dG(x0) distinct neighbors before success is recorded, giving evaluation cost greater than L. Thus WG(q; x0) ≥ L in both cases, proving the theorem. 

3.4 Completing the uniform lower bound 

To complete Theorem 3.1(b), we choose T , L, and ρ so that Theorem 3.3 applies below the critical degree. In particular, dJ must exceed log T and dI must exceed log L by fixed positive multiples of 

d.

Proof of Theorem 3.1(b). The identity cos r∗(θ) = cos 2 θ gives cot 2 θ tan 2(r∗(θ)/2) = cos 2 θ/ (1 + cos 2 θ). Hence Iθ(r∗(θ)) = 12 log(1 + cos 2 θ) and Jθ(r∗(θ)) = 0. For fixed θ, the rate Iθ(ρ) increases with ρ, whereas Jθ(ρ) decreases to zero as ρ increases to r∗(θ). Both rates are continuous on the parameter ranges used below. In the logarithmic regime, set L = ⌈nβ ⌉ with β < β ∗(c). For the limiting angle θ∞ = arcsin( e−1/c )computed in the proof of Theorem 3.1(a), we have cI θ∞ (r∗(θ∞)) = β∗(c) > β . We can therefore choose a fixed ρ < r ∗(θ∞) close enough to the boundary that cI θ∞ (ρ) > β . This choice also gives 

Jθ∞ (ρ) > 0. For T = ⌊na⌋ with 0 < a < 1, the cap estimate gives ϑ → arcsin e−(1 −a)/c . As a ↓ 0, 25 this limit approaches θ∞. By continuity, we can fix a sufficiently small a > 0 so that I > β/c and 

J > a/c with fixed positive margins for all large n. Taking a sufficiently small fixed ctrap > 0 below these margins and below a/c verifies the assumptions on T , I, and J in Theorem 3.3. The remaining angular conditions hold because the angles converge to positive constants below π/ 2 and ρ < r ∗(ϑ). In the dense regime, set L = ⌈eγd ⌉ with γ < γ c. We write ρ = uϑ , where the fixed ratio 0 < u < √2 will be chosen to make I > γ and J > 0. As ϑ → 0, we have 

Iϑ(uϑ ) −→ − 12 log(1 − u2/4) and Jϑ(uϑ ) −→ − 12 log  u2(1 − u2/4) . (16) Choose u so that 2 √1 − e−2γ < u < √2. By (16), I tends to a value larger than γ and J

tends to a positive value. Fix a > 0 smaller than the limit of Jϑ(uϑ ) and set T = ⌊ead ⌋. By (5), sin ϑ = ea sin θ1(1+ O(d−1)), so ϑ → 0. The errors in (16) are O(ϑ2) = o(1). A sufficiently small fixed 

ctrap > 0 therefore verifies the assumptions on T , I, and J in Theorem 3.3, and ρ = uϑ < r ∗(ϑ) for large n. Since u < √2, cot ϑ tan( ρ/ 2) stays a fixed distance below one, as required by Theorem 3.3. With the hypotheses verified in both regimes, Theorem 3.3 applies simultaneously to every navigation graph with probability at least 1 − e−ctrap d/ 4 over Dn. For each graph G, it gives a measurable query set QG with σd−1(QG) ≥ 1−e−ctrap d/ 8. For every q ∈ QG, at least (1 −e−ctrap d/ 8)T

entries x0 satisfy WG(q; x0) ≥ L = ∆. If ∆( G) ≤ ∆, greedy navigation fails from at least (1 − e−ctrap d/ 8)T entries for every q ∈ QG. The data failure probability and the excluded surface measure are n−Ω(1) in the logarithmic regime and e−Ω( d) in the dense regime. Substituting T = ⌊na⌋

or T = ⌊ead ⌋, respectively, and absorbing integer rounding into the o(1) factors proves part (b). 

3.5 Average degree and evaluation cost 

To strengthen Theorem 3.1(b) from many traps to a lower bound on average degree, we relate each point’s degree to the queries for which its rank lies in [ T, 2T ). We show that, with probability 1 −o(1) over the data, every point belongs to this rank window for nearly the same surface measure of queries. Combined with (15), this balance forces most neighbor lists to be long whenever navigation succeeds from every entry for most queries. 

Theorem 3.4 (Degree and evaluation-cost lower bounds) . Fix k ≥ 1 and 0 ≤ ε < 1. For uniform data in either regime, with probability 1 − o(1) over Dn, every navigation graph G that succeeds from every entry on a measurable query set SG with σd−1(SG) ≥ 1 − ε has all but (ε + o(1)) n points of degree at least (∆ ∗)1−o(1) . In particular, its average degree is at least (1 − ε − o(1))(∆ ∗)1−o(1) . For every query in SG outside a set of surface measure o(1) , all but an (ε + o(1)) fraction of entries x0

satisfy WG(q; x0) ≥ (∆ ∗)1−o(1) . The conclusion also holds for ε = εn → 0.Proof. To treat both regimes in one argument, write ( bn, χ ∗) = (log n, β ∗(c)) in the logarithmic regime and ( bn, χ ∗) = ( d, γ c) in the dense regime. Thus bn is the exponent scale and χ∗ is the corresponding critical exponent, with ∆ ∗ = e(χ∗+o(1)) bn . Fix a degree exponent 0 < t < χ ∗ and let 

L = ⌈etb n ⌉. Choose T and ρ as in Section 3.4, using t in place of β or γ. We prove the bounds for this fixed t, then let t approach χ∗.To convert Theorem 3.3 into a degree bound for most data points, we estimate the surface measure of queries for which each point has rank in [ T, 2T ). For x ∈ Dn, let Wx = {q : T ≤ rank q(x) < 2T }

and wx = σd−1(Wx). For any fixed A0 > 0, we will bound all wx simultaneously with failure probability at most n−A0 . Use εrank = p32( A0 + 4) log n/T as the relative slack when comparing ranks with their binomial means. The choices of T give T ≫ log n and T ≤ n/ 3, so εrank = o(1). With px(q) = F (δ(q, x )) as in the proof of Theorem 3.3, define the query sets approximating Wx

26 from inside and outside by 

Ix = {q : (1 + εrank )T ≤ np x(q) ≤ (2 − εrank )T },

Ox = {q : (1 − εrank )T ≤ np x(q) ≤ (2 + εrank )T }.

For fixed x, the variable px(q) is uniform under q ∼ σd−1, giving σd−1(Ix) = (1 − 2εrank )T /n and 

σd−1(Ox) = (1 + 2 εrank )T /n . Conditional on x and q, (8) gives a binomial mean ( n − 1) px(q) for rank q(x) − 1. For q ∈ I x, the event q / ∈ Wx requires a deviation of at least εrank T / 2 from this mean for all large n. For q / ∈ O x, the event q ∈ Wx requires an upper deviation when np x(q) < (1 − εrank )T

and a lower deviation when np x(q) > (2 + εrank )T . Monotonicity bounds the upper tail by its value at the lower endpoint and the lower tail by its value at the upper endpoint. At both endpoints, the required deviation is again at least εrank T / 2. Thus (9) bounds each error probability by 2 e−εrank 2T / 32 .Integrating these error bounds over q by Tonelli’s theorem, with x fixed, shows that the expectation over the remaining n − 1 data points of σd−1(Ix \ Wx) + σd−1(Wx \ O x) is at most 4e−εrank 2T / 32 . Markov’s inequality and a union bound over the data labels show that each such sum is at most εrank T /n simultaneously, except with probability at most 4 n−A0−2/(εrank T ) ≤ n−A0 .Comparing Wx with Ix and Ox therefore gives, with probability at least 1 − n−A0 , simultaneously for every x ∈ Dn, the bounds (1 − 3εrank ) Tn ≤ wx ≤ (1 + 3 εrank ) Tn . (17) We now combine (17) with the bound Y ≤ e−ctrap d/ 4 from the proof of Theorem 3.3. Both bounds hold with probability at least 1 − e−Ω( bn). Fix a data set satisfying both bounds and any graph G that succeeds from every entry on SG. Let DG = {x ∈ Dn : dG(x) ≤ L} be the points with short lists, and form H by retaining G(x) for x ∈ D G and using an empty list elsewhere. For 

q ∈ SG and x ∈ D G with q ∈ Wx, the list H(x) = G(x) must contain an improving point. Otherwise navigation from x would fail, since T > k puts x outside Top k(q). Applying (15) to H therefore bounds the integral over SG of the number of data points x ∈ D G with q ∈ Wx by T Y . Outside SG,the contribution is at most εT , because every query outside the bisectors has exactly T points in the rank window. The integral over the whole query sphere equals P 

> x∈D G

wx and is consequently at most ( Y + ε)T . Combining this upper bound with (17) gives
