Every point outside DG has degree greater than L, so the average degree is at least (1 − ε − o(1)) etb n .The same long lists force an evaluation cost greater than L at the first expansion. For every query q ∈ SG and every entry x / ∈ D G, complete scanning evaluates all dG(x) > L distinct neighbors before success is recorded. Hence WG(q; x) > L for all but an ( ε + o(1)) fraction of entries. For each fixed t < χ ∗, the lower bounds on degree and evaluation cost hold simultaneously for every graph and uniformly in 0 ≤ ε < 1. We may let t approach χ∗ slowly enough that T / log n → ∞ 

and the probability that either (17) or Y ≤ e−ctrap d/ 4 fails still tends to zero. This gives the scale (∆ ∗)1−o(1) in both the degree and evaluation-cost bounds. Uniformity in ε also permits εn → 0. Taking ε = o(1) in Theorem 3.4 gives the critical exponent for both average degree and evaluation cost averaged over entries whenever a graph succeeds from every entry for most uniform queries. Together with Theorem 3.1(a), this completes the uniform critical curve. 27 4 Nonuniform data and the critical degree 

We now extend the uniform results of Section 3 to nonuniform sampling. In the dense regime, the uniform nearest-neighbor scale tends to zero, and local density variation determines a sharp worst-case degree curve over the range in (3). Section 4.1 states this curve and gives the directional comparison used in its upper bound. Section 4.2 proves the upper bound with separate density conditions near and far from the query, while Section 4.3 proves the matching obstruction for every graph. In the logarithmic regime, the uniform nearest-neighbor angle stays bounded away from zero, so the density level also changes the local nearest-neighbor angle and the radius needed for a neighbor list of prescribed length. We therefore treat its upper and lower bounds separately in Sections 4.4 and 4.5. The upper bounds include a graph construction that allows arbitrarily deep low-density regions, while the lower bounds show how sufficiently strong high-density concentration defeats every graph of the prescribed degree. 

4.1 The dense nonuniform critical curve 

To determine the dense curve, we compare the effects of density variation on the probability of an improving lens and on the radius needed to collect a fixed number of neighbors. Under the (κ, K )-regularity conditions of Section 2.5, the comparison gives the boundary κ = h(γ), equivalently 

e2γ = 4 e2κ − 2 by (11). The following theorem gives navigation on N∆ above the curve and a matching obstruction for every navigation graph below it. 

Theorem 4.1 (The dense nonuniform critical curve) . Write κ0 = 12 log(3 /2) for the density endpoint in (3) . In the dense regime, fix k ≥ 1, γ > γ c, and κ ≥ 0, and let ∆ = ⌈eγd ⌉.

(a) (Above the curve.) If 0 ≤ κ ≤ κ0 and κ < h (γ), then for all sufficiently large fixed K

depending on γ and κ, all (κ, K )-regular sampling densities, and all absolutely continuous query distributions Q fixed before the data, with probability 1 − e−Ω( d) over Dn, there is a Borel query set QD with Pq∼Q(q ∈ QD) ≥ 1 − e−Ω( d) such that N∆ succeeds from every entry for every q ∈ QD. For every q ∈ QD, every run uses at most H = O(1 + ρ−1min ) = eo(d) complete expansions and has evaluation cost at most 1 + ∆ H = e(γ+o(1)) d. The implicit constants depend only on k, K, γ, and κ.

(b) (Below the curve.) If γ ≤ log 2 and κ > h (γ), there exist a positive sampling density f for which the essential supremum of log f over the sphere minus its essential infimum is exactly κd and an absolutely continuous query distribution Qn, both fixed before the data, such that the density is 

(κ, K )-regular for every fixed K > 0 and, with probability 1 − e−Ω( d) over Dn, every navigation graph of degree at most ∆ has failing entries for all queries outside a graph-dependent set of 

Qn-probability e−Ω( d). For each such query, at least eΩ( d) entries fail. The query distributions satisfy sup p∈Sd−1 Pq∼Qn (q ∈ C(p, θ )) → 0 for every fixed 0 < θ < π/ 2.

The upper bound is the specialization ¯ κ = κ of the more general navigation theorem in Section 4.2. We give its full proof there, derive part (a), and then choose a degree sequence approaching the curve on one fixed family of density neighborhoods. Part (b) is proved in Section 4.3. The directional comparison used for the upper bound and in Theorem 4.4 compares the tangent direction of a sampled point about q with the opposite tangent direction of a point drawn from P conditional on being closer to q than the sampled point. Taking the opposite direction turns the condition that the closer point misses the neighbor cap into a lower bound on the inner product of the two directions. To state this comparison, let ω be normalized surface measure on the sphere of directions Sd−2.Write Bs, B t ≥ 1 for upper bounds on two probability densities of directions relative to ω. In the 28 application, Bs bounds the density of a sampled point’s tangent direction at q, conditional on its distance from q. The bound Bt applies to the opposite of the tangent direction at q of a point drawn from P conditional on being closer to q than the sampled point. To construct the comparison densities, fix a center e ∈ Sd−2. For any density upper bound B ≥ 1 relative to ω, let EB be the cap centered at e with ω(EB ) = 1 /B , and define hB = B 1EB . Taking B = Bs and B = Bt gives the two comparison densities, supported on caps with the same center. To bound the probability that a closer point misses the current point’s neighbor cap, for a direction density g and 0 < τ < 1define Kτ g(u) = R 1{u · v ≥ τ } g(v) dω (v). This is the probability that a direction drawn from g

has inner product at least τ with a fixed direction u. In the application, u is the tangent direction of the current point at q, and g describes the opposite directions of closer points.

and Cd−2(t) for the corresponding cap probability on Sd−3. Their rate is I(t) = − 12 log(1 − t2) for 0 < t < 1. By Theorem 2.3, Cd−1(t) = exp {− dI (t) + O(log d)} uniformly on compact subsets of (0 , 1), and the same estimate holds for Cd−2(t). 29 4.2 Navigation with a sharper density bound near the query 

To prove Theorem 4.1(a), we separate the density condition near the query from the one needed to reach it. Density variation at most eκd near q, with κ < h (γ), rules out false local minima. Variation at most e¯κd around every possible current point, with ¯ κ < γ , supplies progress from distant entries. The next theorem gives this separation. Taking ¯ κ = κ will yield Theorem 4.1(a). 

Theorem 4.3 (Navigation with a sharper density bound near the query) . In the dense regime fix

To integrate the three progress estimates over queries, we first verify that their failure sets are Borel. Put Wq = C(q, Lρ (q)) and mq = ess inf Wq f . The finite density variation on Wq gives 0 < m q ≤ Mq < ∞. For every real t > 0, consider the maps

Continuity of ρ, the zero surface measure of cap boundaries and domination by f + t make both maps continuous. Their zero sets are respectively {Mq ≤ t} and {mq ≥ t}, so Mq is lower semicontinuous and mq is upper semicontinuous. Hence Qκ = {q : Mq − eκd mq ≤ 0} is closed, and Rq, determined from Mq, is Borel. The property Zcore (q) = 0 and the existence of other sampled points in each set 

V and Vx at its corresponding middle or outer data point are finite distance comparisons. Their failure indicators are therefore measurable. The estimates for each fixed q ∈ Q κ can now be integrated against Q. Let χ∗ = min {I(v0) −

κ1, I (τ − t0v0), I (t0) − A} > 0 and c4 = χ∗/8. The inner cap estimate bounds its failure probability by e−χ∗d+O(log d), while the middle and outer failure probabilities are e−ω(d). Thus the probability that any of the three properties fails is at most e−c4d for all sufficiently large n. Let B consist of the queries in Qκ for which Zcore (q) > 0 or one of those sets V or Vx contains no other sampled point, together with the data bisectors. Tonelli and Markov at threshold e−c4d/ 2 give, with probability at least 1 − e−c4d/ 2, an exceptional set B of Q-probability at most e−c4d/ 2, since the bisectors have 

Q-probability zero. Combining this conclusion with retention and taking c∗ = χ∗/32 gives the stated data probability and exceptional query probability for all sufficiently large n.Fix a data set for which retention holds and B has the stated probability bound. For any 

q ∈ Q κ \ B and any entry, outer moves decrease the query distance by at least cpρmin . Middle moves multiply it by at most b over a distance ratio DR/ Θ ≤ D/ (ac 0). These two regions therefore require 

O(1 + ρ−1min ) moves. 34 Inside the inner cap, every list contains the radius-R edges. Any two path vertices separated by at least two moves must be more than R apart, since the later vertex would otherwise be a listed neighbor closer to q than the immediate successor actually chosen. In each parity subsequence, the Euclidean separation exceeds 2 sin( R/ 2), while all vertices lie within distance 2 sin(Θ /2) of q. The squared ratio of these radii is 1 − cos Θ 1 − cos R = a2 1 + cos R

1 + cos Θ ≤ a2 < 12 .

The pairwise squared-distance identity in the proof of Theorem 3.2 bounds each subsequence by 

⌈(1 − 2a2)−1⌉ vertices. The absence of false local minima forces success, so the total number of complete expansions is H = O(1 + ρ−1min ) = eo(d), including the final expansion and the expansion of an entry that is already the nearest neighbor. The evaluation cost is at most 1 + ∆ H. For fixed 

k ≥ 1, success occurs no later than for k = 1, because the nearest neighbor belongs to Top k(q). The same complete-expansion and cost bounds therefore apply. 

Proof of Theorem 4.1(a). Finite log-density oscillation on C(x, Ke γ ρ(x)), a cap containing positive 

P -mass, makes its essential density bounds finite and positive. A finite subcover of these caps gives 

f > 0 almost everywhere on the sphere. Take ¯ κ = κ in Theorem 4.3 and choose K ≥ K0(γ, κ, κ ). Every query then belongs to Qκ, giving the stated bounds. To obtain the degree scale in (3), the density condition must remain on one fixed neighborhood while the degree approaches the curve. For fixed 0 ≤ κ ≤ κ0, take γj = 12 log(4 e2κ − 2) + 1 /j . In the proof of Theorem 4.3 with ¯ κ = κ, we have g − κ > γ c, so w = 1 /4 and J = 16. The parameters 

g, κ 1, D and eγj K0(γj , κ, κ ) remain bounded as j → ∞ , including at κ = κ0. Choose one fixed multiplier L larger than all the required values of eγj K0, and assume the density bound on every cap C(x, Lρ (x)). For exponent γj , apply Theorem 4.3 with K = Le −γj , so the assumed caps are unchanged. For each fixed j, the radius comparisons and probability estimates depend only on κ, γ j , L and 

n, d , uniformly over the density class and the query distribution. In particular, the contradiction establishing ρmax < π/L uses only 1 /n ≥ e−κd F (π/L ), so its threshold is independent of the density. Choose increasing deterministic thresholds Nj so that, for all n ≥ Nj , both error probabilities are at most 1 /j and the constant multiplying 1 + ρ−1min in the expansion bound is at most ed/j . Use degree 

⌈eγj d⌉ for Nj ≤ n < N j+1 . This gives one degree sequence for the entire density class. For each density sequence satisfying log(1 /ρ min ) = o(d), its degree and evaluation cost are e( 12 log(4 e2κ−2)+ o(1)) d.The resulting sample probability and query probability are 1 − o(1). Together with Theorem 4.1(b) and Theorem 3.1(b) at κ = 0, this proves the sharp worst-case scale throughout (3). 

4.3 Below the dense curve 

To match the upper bound with an obstruction for every graph, we prove Theorem 4.1(b) by constructing many possible entries in balls surrounded by annuli of lower density. Few improving points lie in those annuli. For stored edges longer than a distance cutoff specified below and ending outside the ball and its surrounding annulus, only a small fraction of query directions allow improvement. The sample estimates will hold before any graph is chosen. For each graph of degree at most ∆, we then bound the exceptional query directions for its stored edges. Repeating the construction across the sphere gives the query distribution. 

Proof of Theorem 4.1(b). We first choose a geometric configuration that balances the number of possible entries against the fraction of query directions for which a fixed stored edge can improve an 35 entry. We then perturb it to obtain strict margins. Use the directional rate I and cap probability 

Cd−1 from Section 4.1. Choose γ1 > γ sufficiently close that h(γ1) < κ , using the formula in (11) also when γ1 > log 2. The gaps γ1 − γ and κ − h(γ1) will leave positive margins in the edge and density estimates. The configuration consists of a ball containing possible entries, an annulus of lower density around it, and closer points beyond the annulus. We first use Euclidean coordinates with the outer annulus radius normalized to one, then scale to the sphere. Fix an axis through the center on which the query will lie. A representative entry and a representative closer point have axial coordinates 

x and B, each at perpendicular distance L from that axis. To specify the configuration, put 

E = e2γ1 > 2 and Z0 = E2 − E − 1, and take x = Z−1/20 , L = p(E − 2) /Z 0, rsrc = p(E − 1) /Z 0,and B = ( E − 1) /√Z0. Here rsrc is the radius of the ball containing the entries. Place the query at axial coordinate t0 = ( B + x)/2. Its common distance to the two representative points is D0 =pL2 + ( B − x)2/4, and the typical distance between such points with independent perpendicular directions is ℓ0 = p(B − x)2 + 2 L2. These choices satisfy rsrc 2 = x2 + L2 < 1, B2 + L2 = 1, 0 < x < t 0 < B < 1, 0 < L < r src , and t0 + D0 < 2. To compare the numbers of points and stored neighbors, set a = − log L and ε = log( rsrc /L ), and choose the common angular scale R by nF (R) = ead . The dense regime and Theorem 2.1 give 

R → 0 and log(1 /R ) = o(d). For fixed positive radius multipliers, the same estimate gives 

F (vR )/F (R) = ed log v+o(d) (22) uniformly on compact ranges of v > 0. After scaling to the sphere, the outer cap has angular radius R and the source cap has angular radius rsrc R. Under uniform sampling their expected numbers of points are ead and e(ε+o(1)) d, respectively. The directional estimate below has exponent 

J = 12 log((1 + x2)/L 2). The two relations that connect these exponents to the density and degree are 

a + log D0 = 12 log E + 2 4 = h(γ1) and J − ε = γ1. (23) The inequality a + log D0 < κ will make the expected number of sampled points in the low density part of the improving region exponentially small. For candidate neighbors outside the outer cap and separated from the entry by at least the distance cutoff, we bound the fraction of query directions placing both endpoints of a stored edge within the query cap. The inequality J − ε > γ will keep this bound exponentially small after summing over the possible entries and their neighbor lists. We first establish the direction bound for one fixed edge. First fix normalized vectors X, Y ∈ Rd−1 for a source and a candidate neighbor outside the unit ball, with |X| = rsrc , |Y | = r ≥ 1, and |X − Y | ≥ ℓ0. A unit query direction u that places both points within distance D0 of the query satisfies |t0u − X| ≤ D0 and |t0u − Y | ≤ D0. These bounds give u · X ≥ x and u · Y ≥ B + ( r2 − 1) /(B + x). To combine the projection requirements into one cap condition, put V = xX + BY , s0 = x2 + B2, and z = r2 − 1. The projection of V and its squared norm satisfy 

u · V ≥ s0 + BB + x z =: S(z),

|V |2 ≤ x2rsrc 2 + B2r2 + xB (rsrc 2 + r2 − ℓ20) = s0(1 + x2) + B(B + x)z =: N (z).

For z ≥ 0, both S(z) and N (z) are positive. Thus S(z)2/N (z) is a lower bound on the squared cosine threshold of the cap about V / |V |. The derivative of S(z)2/N (z) has the sign of 2 BN (z)/(B +

x) − B(B + x)S(z), an affine function with positive slope and constant term s0Bℓ 20/(B + x) > 0. The minimum is therefore attained at z = 0, giving squared threshold at least s0/(1 + x2) = 1 − e−2J .36 We need this direction bound both at the initial parameters and when the source radius and query distance vary slightly. Write s = |X| for the normalized radius of the source and r = |Y | for that of the candidate neighbor, t′ for the normalized query distance from the center, and D for their common distance bound from the query. Let ℓ be the distance cutoff between nearby and farther pairs, to be chosen below ℓ0, and reserve an exponent margin ζ = ( γ1 − γ)/8. The corresponding bounds on the projection and squared norm are 

S(s, r, t ′, D ) = x(t′2 + s2 − D2) + B(t′2 + r2 − D2)2t′ ,

N (s, r, ℓ ) = x2s2 + B2r2 + xB (s2 + r2 − ℓ2).

At ( s, t ′, D, ℓ ) = ( rsrc , t 0, D 0, ℓ 0), the preceding calculation holds uniformly for 1 ≤ r ≤ 2. By continuity and compactness, choose a fixed neighborhood of this tuple on which 

S, N > 0, S2/N > 1 − e−2( J−ζ/ 2) , and t′ + D < 2 (24) uniformly for 1 ≤ r ≤ 2. Choose a distance cutoff ℓ < ℓ 0 in this neighborhood. To apply the direction estimate on the sphere, identify the tangent space at a center p with 

Rd−1. For a tangent vector X, exp p(X) denotes travel from p by distance |X| in direction X/ |X|,with exp p(0) = p. Here | · | is Euclidean norm. Write the query as q = exp p(t′Ru ) and the two points as exp p(RX ) and exp p(RY ). If the second point is outside C(p, R ) and within distance DR 

of q, then 1 ≤ | Y | ≤ t′ + D < 2. Expanding the spherical cosine identity for the two points at R = 0 gives, uniformly for |X|, |Y | ≤ 2, the metric comparison 

R−2δ(exp p(RX ), exp p(RY )) 2 = |X − Y |2 + O(R2). (25) For pairs at mutual distance at least ℓR , (24) therefore gives a direction cap with squared threshold at least 1 − e−2( J−ζ/ 2) + O(R2). Since R → 0 in the dense regime, this threshold is at least 1 − e−2( J−ζ) for large n. The fraction of query directions placing both points within distance DR of 

q is consequently at most exp {− (J − ζ + o(1)) d}. (26) A threshold at least one gives zero direction measure. The errors are uniform over the fixed parameter neighborhoods and all such pairs, so (26) applies after the data and graph have been chosen. For pairs closer than ℓR , we bound the probability that such a pair appears in the sample. We first enclose the region containing an entry and the region containing a closer exterior point in small caps. Suppose δ(p, q ) = t′R. Let rR be the radius of the cap about p and DR the permitted 37 distance from q, with r, D > 0 fixed. In this containment calculation, s = ( t′2 + r2 − D2)/(2 t′) is the axial coordinate of the containing cap center in units of R, and LS = √r2 − s2 is its leading radius multiplier. At r = rs, t′ = t0, and D = D0, these are the quantities ss and Ls defined above. When 0 < s < min {r, t ′}, the intersection C(p, rR ) ∩ C(q, DR ) lies in a cap with center at distance sR + O(R3) from p toward q and radius ( LS + O(R2)) R. To see this, combine the two cosine inequalities with weights 1 − s/t ′ and s/t ′, then normalize the vector (1 − s/t ′)p + ( s/t ′)q.The resulting cosine threshold is (1 − s/t ′) cos( rR ) + ( s/t ′) cos( DR )

p1 − 2( s/t ′)(1 − s/t ′)(1 − cos( t′R)) = 1 − 12 (r2 − s2)R2 + O(R4).

Expanding the normalized vector along the geodesic from p to q gives the cap center’s location. Both expansions are uniform on compact parameter sets satisfying the strict conditions. The second point lies in the exterior region C(q, DR ) \ C(p, R ). For this region, put b′ =(t′2 + 1 − D2)/(2 t′) and LT = √1 − b′2, and suppose t′ < b ′ < 1. The region C(q, DR ) \ C(p, R ) is contained in a cap centered at distance b′R from p toward q, with radius ( LT + O(R2)) R. Indeed, the unit center is z = αq −βp , where α = sin( b′R)/ sin( t′R) > 0 and β = α cos( t′R)−cos( b′R) > 0. Every 

y ∈ C(q, DR )\C(p, R ) satisfies z ·y ≥ α cos( DR )−β cos R. The right side is 1 − 12 (1 −b′2)R2 +O(R4), using D2 − t′2 − 1 = −2t′b′, which proves the radius bound. At ( r, t ′, D ) = ( rsrc , t 0, D 0), the two containing caps have radii ( L + O(R2)) R and centers separated by ( B − x + O(R2)) R. Independent uniform points in them typically have squared distance close to ℓ20R2, so pairs within distance ℓR have exponentially small probability. For the needed uniform bound, choose τ ∈ (0 , 1/4) sufficiently small that (B − x)2 + 2(1 − τ )2L2 − 2τ {2( B − x)L + L2} > ℓ 2. (27) This inequality remains strict when the two radii and the distance between their centers vary in a sufficiently small compact neighborhood. In either cap, the probability that a uniform point’s radial distance is less than 1 − τ times the cap radius is exp {(log(1 − τ ) + o(1)) d}. Conditional on the radii, the unit tangent directions are independent and uniform in their respective tangent spheres. Regard both as vectors in Rd and take their projections onto the unit direction along the chord joining the two cap centers. Each projection has absolute value at most τ , except with probability 2 Cd−1(τ ). The same probability bound holds for the inner product of the two tangent directions in Rd, conditional on the first direction, because its projection onto the second tangent space has norm at most one. When both points have radial distance at least 1 − τ times their respective cap radii and all three scalar products have absolute value at most τ , expanding the radial representations bounds squared Euclidean distance divided by R2 from below by the left side of (27), up to O(R2). The cross terms involving the line joining the centers decrease this lower bound by at most 4 τ (B − x)L, and the cross term between the two directions decreases it by at most 2 τ L 2. These bounds vary continuously in the chosen neighborhood. Geodesic distance is at least Euclidean distance. Thus the probability of distance at most ℓR is at most e−cpd for some fixed cp > 0, uniformly in that neighborhood. One may take cp = 116 min {− log(1 − τ ), I (τ )} for sufficiently large n.To transfer this pair bound to the sample, shrink the parameter neighborhood until a + log LS <cp/8 and a+log LT < c p/8, which is possible because both left sides vanish at ( r, t ′, D ) = ( rsrc , t 0, D 0). We now combine the upper bound on Ni, the lower bound on |Si(q)|, the exclusions in (28) and (33) to (35), and the existence of k closer sampled points. For a fixed data set, let A(Dn)be the Qn-probability of queries violating at least one of these requirements. The requirements defining A(Dn) are finite distance and cardinality comparisons, so their failure set is Borel. The fixed-query bounds are uniform in i, t ′, u . Integrating over t′, u and summing with weights wi gives 

EA(Dn) ≤ e−Ω( d) by Tonelli and P 

> i

wi = 1. Markov yields a data event of probability 1 − e−Ω( d)

on which A(Dn) ≤ e−Ω( d). This event is determined before any graph is chosen. Fix a data set with A(Dn) ≤ e−Ω( d), and let G be any graph of degree at most ∆. The preceding sample estimates exclude improving neighbors outside Si(q) unless the listed point lies outside 

C(pi, R ) and is at distance at least ℓR from its source. We now exclude queries for which such a listed point lies closer to q. For each center with Ni ≤ e(ε+ζ)d, the number of listed neighbors across all data points in Si is at most Ni∆ ≤ e(ε+ζ+γ+o(1)) d. Consider a pair whose source has distance in [rsR, r src R] from pi, whose listed neighbor lies outside C(pi, R ), and whose mutual distance is at least ℓR . For each fixed t′, (26) bounds the fraction of directions placing both endpoints within distance d+R of the query. A union bound over the listed neighbors gives at most 

e(ε+γ+2 ζ−J+o(1)) d = e(−6ζ+o(1)) d.

Integrating this direction fraction over t′ and summing with weights wi gives the same bound on the probability of the additional excluded queries. That query set may depend on G, but its bound holds for every G on the fixed data event. The selected sources at radius below rsR are already absent by (34), and nearby pairs leaving C(pi, R ) are already excluded by (28). Every improving listed neighbor of a point in Si(q) lies within distance d+R of the query, so these exclusions cover all ways to reach a listed neighbor outside C(pi, R ). For a query satisfying the sample requirements used to define A(Dn) and outside the additional excluded set for G, every improving stored edge from Si(q) ends in Si(q). A sampled point of Si

outside Si(q) is farther than d+R from the query. By (35), C(q, d +R) ∩ (C(pi, R ) \ Si) contains no sampled point, and the pair estimates exclude improving listed neighbors outside C(pi, R ). Thus the finite set Si(q), of size at least edsd/ 2, cannot be left by strictly improving moves and contains no member of Top k(q). Every run starting there eventually stops without success. Scanning entire lists cannot reveal a target either, because every member of Top k(q) is closer than every sampled point of Si and would therefore be an improving listed neighbor. 41 The bounds on Ni and |Si(q)|, the exclusions in (28) and (33) to (35), the existence of k closer points in Ti(q), and the final union bound for listed neighbors all have fixed positive exponential margins, uniformly for t′ ∈ [t − w, t + w]. Choose c∗ > 0 smaller than one hundredth of these margins, including the exponent ds/2 for the number of selected sources. For sufficiently large n,the uniform lower order errors and the factors from Markov and the final union bound are absorbed into the margins. The data event then has probability at least 1 − e−c∗d, and on it every graph of degree at most ∆ has at least ec∗d failing entries for every query outside a set of Qn-probability at most e−c∗d. This proves Theorem 4.1(b). 

4.4 Upper bounds in the logarithmic regime 

We now consider the logarithmic regime, where the uniform nearest-neighbor angle converges to a positive constant. The upper bound for N∆ depends on both the density level and its variation. We first quantify that dependence, then construct a different graph that, for densities bounded above by eo(d), succeeds from every entry for most uniform queries even with arbitrarily deep low-density regions. Let d = ⌊c log n⌋, β∗(c) < β < 1, ∆ = ⌈nβ ⌉, λ = 1 /c and γ = β/c , so that n = eλd +O(1) . To describe a region where f has exponential level eμd , write ν = λ + μ > 0. In the cap estimate of Theorem 2.1, this level multiplies the expected number of sampled points by eμd . For 0 ≤ g < ν ,let R(g, ν ) be the limiting radius of a cap containing egd points in expectation at this density level. Thus R(0 , ν ) is the limiting nearest-neighbor angle. We use the ratio X(g, ν ) = (1 −

cos R(g, ν )) / sin 2 R(0 , ν ) to compare the neighbor radius with that nearest-neighbor scale. The cap estimate gives the following formulas for R and X, and we define the permitted density variation h

by 

R(g, ν ) = arcsin eg−ν , X(g, ν ) = e2g

1 + cos R(g, ν ) , and h(g, ν ) = 12 log 1 + X(g, ν )2 .

For ν = λ, R(γ, λ ) = lim ρβ and R(0 , λ ) = lim θ1. The condition X(γ, λ ) > 1 is equivalent to cos R(γ, λ ) < cos 2 R(0 , λ ), the limiting critical-radius inequality in (1). Hence h(γ, λ ) > 0 exactly when β > β ∗(c). As the density level μ increases, both the local nearest-neighbor angle and the local neighbor-list radius decrease, and X(γ, ν ) decreases in ν, so denser regions are harder to navigate at the same degree. Fix a sequence ϑd = o(d) with ϑd ≥ log 4. For μ+, μ − ≥ 0, let D(μ+, μ −) consist of probability densities satisfying −μ−d − ϑd ≤ log f ≤ μ+d + ϑd almost everywhere. The term ϑd

absorbs subexponential factors in the density bounds. The next theorem expresses the sufficient condition using the largest allowed density level and the global density ratio.

We now combine the fixed-query estimates and bound the length of the run. Integrating the fixed-query failure bounds against Q and applying Markov’s inequality, as in the proof of Theorem 4.3, gives, with probability 1 − e−Ω( d) over the data, the required progress for all queries outside a set of Q-measure e−Ω( d). contractions. Inside, vertices separated by at least two moves are more than Rn apart, and they lie in the Euclidean ball of radius sin Θ n about cos Θ n q. Thus the number of moves and complete expansions is 

O(1), and the evaluation cost is O(∆). For the class D(μ+, μ −), take M = eμ+d+ϑd , ¯ μ = μ+ and κ = μ+ + μ−. The sufficient condition is then μ+ + μ− < h (γ, λ + μ+). When the global density ratio is eo(d), normalization permits ¯μ = κ = 0 after absorbing that variation into ϑd.For densities with ess sup f / ess inf f = no(1) , the theorem gives the same upper degree exponent 

β∗(c) as for uniform data. This guarantee for N∆ uses both upper and lower density bounds. If only the upper density is bounded by a subexponential factor, changing the graph still gives the uniform upper degree exponent, even when the density vanishes in some regions. The construction uses a fixed number of nested subsamples. Every neighbor list contains a common small subsample and nearby points from the larger subsamples. To make progress through these subsamples, we need a lower bound on the probability mass of query caps despite the absence of a lower density bound. The next lemma supplies it when the query density is also bounded by a subexponential factor, including uniform queries and queries drawn from P .

Lemma 4.5 (Sampling mass of query caps) . Let Pd = fdσd−1 be a probability law with 0 ≤ fd ≤ eϑd

a.e., where ϑd is any fixed sequence satisfying ϑd = o(d). Fix 0 < a < b < π/ 2 and ξ > 0. Let Qd be a deterministic query distribution with a density satisfying dQ d/dσ d−1 ≤ eωd a.e., where ωd = o(d).There is b0 > 0 such that, for all sufficiently large d, there exists a Borel set Ed, depending only on 

Pd, with Pq∼Qd (q ∈ Ed) ≤ e−b0d such that 

Pd(C(q, t )) ≥ e−ξd F (t) (q / ∈ Ed, a ≤ t ≤ b). (36) 

The constants may depend on a, b, ξ . The threshold for sufficiently large d may also depend on the sequences in the hypotheses. Proof. We first obtain the bound for uniform queries at one fixed radius D ∈ (0 , π/ 2), allowing an exponent loss ζ > 0. Choose u ∈ (0 , D ) sufficiently small that log sin( D − u) > log sin D − ζ/ 2. Let 

g(z) = Pd(C(z, D − u)) and Ad = {z : g(z) ≥ F (D − u)/2}. Fubini gives R g dσ d−1 = F (D − u), while g ≤ eϑd F (D − u), so σd−1(Ad) ≥ 12 e−ϑd = e−o(d). The function g is continuous by dominated convergence, since cap boundaries have Pd-measure zero. Thus Ad is compact. Spherical isoperimetry says that, among closed sets of a given surface measure, a spherical cap minimizes the measure of points at distance at most u from the set, for every fixed u > 0 [FLM77, Theorem 2.1]. By Theorem 2.1, a cap of measure at least e−o(d) has radius at least π/ 2−u/ 2 for all large d. Consequently the set of points at distance greater than u from every point of Ad has measure at most 

F (π/ 2 − u/ 2) = exp  d log cos( u/ 2) + O(log d).

a = t0 < · · · < t L = b with log sin ti+1 − log sin ti < ξ/ 8. Apply the preceding step with exponent loss ξ/ 8 to its finitely many radii. For any ti ≤ t ≤ ti+1 , monotonicity of cap mass and Theorem 2.1 give

Sd−1 × [a, b ] by dominated convergence. The set where (36) fails for some t is Borel, since one may test a countable dense set of radii. Taking this failure set as Ed completes the proof. 

Theorem 4.6 (Navigation by a graph built from nested subsamples) . Fix c > 0, β∗(c) < β < 1,and k ≥ 1. Let d = ⌊c log n⌋ and ∆ = ⌈nβ ⌉. For every sequence of probability laws Pn = fnσd−1

The total move bound J + 2 ⌈K⌉ + 2 is independent of n, and the evaluation cost is at most 1 + ( J + 2 ⌈K⌉ + 3)∆. The degree exponent is sharp over D(0 , κ ) under uniform queries for every fixed κ ≥ 0, since the class includes uniform sampling and Theorem 3.1(b) applies below β∗(c). 

4.5 Lower bounds in the logarithmic regime 

The preceding construction handles arbitrarily low density by changing the graph. We now ask when the density instead forces every graph to use long neighbor lists. We first show that subexponential global density variation preserves the uniform lower exponent, then construct high density concentrations that force a strictly larger exponent. 

Corollary 4.7 (The uniform lower exponent under small global variation) . Fix k ≥ 1, c > 0, and 

0 < β < β ∗(c) in the logarithmic regime, and let ∆ = ⌈nβ ⌉. Suppose that Kn = ess sup f / ess inf f =

no(1) and that the ratio of the query density to f is at most no(1) , with the query distribution fixed before sampling. There are constants a, η > 0 such that, with probability at least 1 − n−η over Dn,every navigation graph G has a measurable query set of Q-probability at least 1 − n−η on which at least (1 − o(1)) ⌊na⌋ entries x0 satisfy WG(q; x0) ≥ ∆. From each such entry whose neighbor list has length at most ∆, greedy navigation fails to evaluate a member of Top k(q).Proof. Use the logarithmic-regime choices of T = ⌊na⌋, L = ⌈nβ ⌉, cutoff radius ρ, and rates I, J 

from the proof of Theorem 3.1(b). Normalization gives K−1 

> n

≤ f ≤ Kn. Choose Jn = no(1) with 

dQ/dP ≤ Jn, so integration against Q is bounded by JnKn times surface integration. By the binomial rank estimate in the proof of Theorem 3.3, the rank interval [ T, 2T ) corresponds, up to an error e−c1T for an absolute c1 > 0, to caps with P -probability in [ T / (4 n), 4T /n ]. Their surface probabilities therefore lie in W∗ = [ T / (4 nK n), 4KnT /n ]. By Theorem 2.1, enlarging the interval to W∗ changes the logarithms of its sine radii by O((1 + log Kn)/d ). The derivative bounds in the proof of Theorem 3.3 consequently change dI and dJ by only O(1 + log Kn) = o(d). Replacing surface probabilities by P -probabilities and surface integration by Q-integration contributes factors eo(d), since JnK4 

> n

= eo(d). The analogue of Y in (15), with cap probabilities taken under P and query integrals under Q, thus has expectation at most 

nT e−c1T + eo(d) T e −dJ + Le −dI  = n−Ω(1) .

The fixed margins in dJ − log T and dI − log L absorb these factors. Markov’s inequality over the sample gives one bound valid for all graphs with lists of length at most L, with probability 1 − n−Ω(1) .For each such graph, Markov’s inequality over queries leaves (1 − o(1)) T points in the rank interval with no improving listed neighbor outside a graph-dependent query set of Q-probability n−Ω(1) .Their ranks exceed k, and no point of Top k(q) can be in such a list, so the run stops without success after its first complete expansion. For an unrestricted graph, retain its lists of length at most L and replace longer lists by empty ones. Each resulting failing entry either still fails in the original graph or has a first complete expansion costing more than L. This proves both assertions. The preceding lower bound matches the uniform exponent when the global density variation is subexponential. To force a larger degree, we concentrate most of the sampling mass in a cap while leaving exponentially many data points outside it. For every graph of the degree allowed below, most uniform queries have many exterior entries with no improving route into the cap. The 48 construction is governed by a degree boundary Γ( μ, λ ) and a required density variation Kcap (μ, λ ), where μ > 0 is the exponent of the density inside the cap. For λ > 0, define Γ( μ, λ ) = λ + 12 log

A degree ∆ = eγd with γ < Γ( μ, λ ) leaves too few stored edges to reach the enriched cap from all exterior entries. The condition κ > K cap (μ, λ ) allows enough exterior points while keeping the log-density oscillation below κd . These are the two conditions in the following theorem, and 

Kcap (μ, λ ) > μ ensures that the stated density class is defined. 

Theorem 4.8 (Enrichment and arbitrary navigation graphs) . Fix c > 0, β∗(c) < β < 1, and k ≥ 1,and put d = ⌊c log n⌋, λ = 1 /c , γ = β/c , and ∆ = ⌈nβ ⌉. Fix μ > 0 and κ > 0. If γ < Γ( μ, λ ) and 

κ > K cap (μ, λ ), there are deterministic, everywhere positive two-level densities fn in D(μ, κ − μ)

with global log-density oscillation at most κd and ess sup fn = e(μ+o(1)) d such that the following holds for Qn = σd−1. With probability 1 − e−Ω( d) over Dn, simultaneously for every navigation graph with neighbor lists of at most ∆ points, navigation fails from eΩ( d) entries for every query outside a Borel set of query measure e−Ω( d) that may depend on the graph. For each such query, a set of eΩ( d)

failing entries is disjoint from Top k(q) and closed under strictly improving greedy moves. Proof. We construct a cap containing most sampling mass and select exterior entries that are farther from q than at least k points in that cap. To make those entries fail for every graph of degree at most ∆, we exclude nearby improving pairs by a sampling estimate and bound, for each farther stored edge, the measure of queries for which it improves the distance. The number of exterior points will be small enough to sum this directional bound over all their stored edges. We use the directional rate I and direction-cap probability Cd−1 defined in Section 4.1 for these estimates. Fix the cap center p and write T = e−μ for the sine of its limiting angular radius B = arcsin T .Set b = √1 − e−2λ and s = T b . For queries orthogonal to p, s is the limiting largest query inner product among sampled points in the cap: among e(λ+o(1)) d independent tangent directions, the largest cosine with a fixed direction approaches b, while the cap boundary has sine T . We will choose the exterior-entry thresholds just below s. By Theorem 2.1, the surface measure of {x : q · x ≥ s}

has rate ϵ0 = I(s) ∈ (0 , λ ). Let 0 < ϵ < ϵ 0 be an exponent to be chosen below for the number of exterior points. For large d, define the radius Bn ∈ (0 , π/ 2) of the enriched cap by F (Bn) = e−μd ,so Bn → B by Theorem 2.1. With the center p fixed above, define the background density pn and the sampling density fn by 

pn = e−(λ−ϵ)d, and fn(x) = pn + (1 − pn)eμd 1{δ(p,x )≤Bn}. (42) This integrates to one and satisfies pn ≤ fn ≤ eμd . Its upper level is eμd (1 − o(1)), and its log-density oscillation is at most ( λ + μ − ϵ)d. Choosing λ + μ − ϵ < κ will give pn > e −(κ−μ)d and place this density in D(μ, κ − μ) without the subexponential slack. Let An = C(p, B n) be the enriched cap and Acn the source region. The density on Acn is exactly pn, while the density on An lies between  

> 12

eμd and eμd for all large n.We first derive the geometric estimates for queries orthogonal to p and exterior points x satisfying 

p · x = 0. The inequalities on ϵ needed for the density class will be verified when it is chosen below. For a point y ∈ C(p, B ) with x · y ≤ s2, an improving edge from x to y when q · x ≥ s must also satisfy q · y ≥ s. These two constraints can be combined with positive weights a = T 2 − s2 and 1 − s2. Their weighted vector is ax + (1 − s2)( y − (p · y)p), whose projection onto q is at least 49 (a + 1 − s2)s. Since |y − (p · y)p| ≤ T , its squared norm is at most N0 below. The allowed query directions therefore lie in a cap with positive cosine threshold z0, where

For each fixed q ∈ B , the sampling estimates give exponentially many entries in S(q) ⊆ Acn and at least k points in An closer to q than every entry in S(q). They also exclude every improving edge of length at most ℓ from S(q) into An. Their combined failure probability is e−Ω( d), uniformly in that query. The failure indicator is jointly Borel, since it uses finitely many comparisons of distances and inner products. Tonelli’s theorem therefore bounds the expected surface measure of queries in 

B where any of these properties fails by e−Ω( d). Markov’s inequality over the sample bounds this exceptional query measure by e−Ω( d) on one data event of probability 1 − e−Ω( d). Intersect that event with the bound NS ≤ e(ϵ+ζ)d, and add Bc to the exceptional queries. The data event and the initial exceptional query set are defined without choosing a graph. Fix such a data set and any graph with degree at most ∆. The neighbor lists of all NS sampled points outside An contain at most NS ∆ ≤ e(ϵ+ζ+γ+o(1)) d listed neighbors. Apply (49) to every stored edge from Acn into An with |p · x| ≤ η and distance at least ℓ. Outside the initial query exception, every point of S(q) satisfies |p · x| ≤ η. For each fixed graph, exclude the queries admitting a stored edge of length at least ℓ from S(q) into An at a point y with q · y ≥ r. Integrating (49) over z = p · q

and summing over all eligible stored edges bounds this additional set’s surface measure by 

e(ϵ+γ+3 ζ−J+o(1)) d = e−Ω( d).

The exponent is negative because ϵ < ϵ 0 and 16 ζ < J − ϵ0 − γ. Both the initial and the additional exceptional query sets have surface measure e−Ω( d).For every query outside the combined exception, the lower bound |S(q)| ≥ edsd/ 2 supplies exponentially many selected sources. An improving neighbor in Acn remains in S(q) because its query inner product remains at least r. An improving neighbor y ∈ An would also satisfy q · y ≥ r.The bound (48) for nearby pairs excludes such edges of length at most ℓ, and (49) excludes longer stored edges outside the additional query exception. Thus S(q) is closed under strictly improving greedy moves. Every sampled point outside An satisfies q · x < u , while (47) supplies at least k

sampled points in An with q · y > u . Hence Top k(q) ⊆ An and S(q) contains no point of Top k(q). A complete expansion at any point of S(q) cannot evaluate a top-k point either, since that point would be an improving neighbor in An. Every run starting in S(q) therefore fails. Exclude also the finite union of bisectors, which has surface measure zero. The geometric estimates are uniform over the fixed closed parameter neighborhoods, and Bn → B, so all stated probability bounds hold for sufficiently large n. This proves the theorem. To express the obstruction directly in terms of degree and density variation, write μmin for the lower bound on the density exponent μ required by this construction. The condition γ < Γ( μ, λ ) is equivalent to μ > μ min , where 

μmin = 12 log (1 − e−2λ)2

1 − e−2( λ−γ) .

The inequality γ > 12 log(2 − e−2λ) gives μmin > 0. The identity Kcap (μ, λ ) = 12 log {1 + e2λ(e2μ − 1) }

shows that Kcap is strictly increasing in μ. Substituting μmin therefore shows that an enrichment level satisfying both theorem conditions exists whenever 

κ > K cap (μmin , λ ) = 12 log (e2λ − 1)( e2γ − 1) 

e2λ − e2γ .

Since Γ( μ, λ ) ↑ λ as μ → ∞ , this construction gives an obstruction for every β∗(c) < β < 1 when the permitted density variation is sufficiently large, with uniform queries throughout. 53 5 Relaxing the navigation guarantee 

Sections 3 and 4 establish bounds for greedy navigation to find a member of Top k(q) from every entry. On threshold and nearest-neighbor graphs, we now ask how much evaluation cost can be saved by allowing fresh random entries, and whether adaptive local exploration with caching can improve on independent restarts. The main result of this section answers these questions for uniform data in the dense regime. After proving it, we return to a guarantee from every entry and relax exact recovery instead, bounding the stopping rank in Section 5.5. For uniform data in the dense regime, let ∆ = ⌈eγd ⌉ with 12 log(4 /3) ≤ γ < γ c. The success probability of one greedy run from a uniform random entry is governed by the exponent 

η∗(γ) = − 12 log 



1 − (2 − e2γ )2(3 e2γ − 2) 2

16 e4γ (e2γ − 1) 



. (50) For any query q fixed before sampling, one greedy run on either Grγ or N∆ succeeds with probability 

e−(η∗(γ)+ o(1)) d when we draw the uniform data and independently choose a uniform entry label (see Theorem 5.3). To determine the cost of attaining constant success probability, we compare independent restarts with the local exploration algorithms introduced before Theorem 1.3. The following theorem gives matching upper and lower bounds on the evaluation cost of fresh starts. 

Theorem 5.1 (The evaluation cost of fresh starts) . For uniform data in the dense regime, fix k ≥ 1

and 12 log(4 /3) ≤ γ < γ c, and let ∆ = ⌈eγd ⌉. For each query q fixed before sampling, we average success probabilities over the random sample and the algorithm’s random choices, including any random entries. On either Grγ or N∆, every local exploration algorithm with data-independent entries requires at least e(γ+η∗(γ)−o(1)) d distinct evaluations to succeed with probability bounded away from zero. Independent restarts from uniform random entries on Grγ , all using the same sample, succeed with probability tending to one using at most e(γ+η∗(γ)+ o(1)) d distinct evaluations. The minimum of γ + η∗(γ) over the stated degree range is attained at e2γ = (12 + 2 √3) /11 and is approximately 0.243 , compared with the every-entry exponent γc ≈ 0.347 .

To prove Theorem 5.1, we first analyze the improving regions and bound the number of expansions in a run from a random entry in Section 5.1. We use these estimates to determine the single-run success exponent and bound adaptive local exploration in Section 5.2. Section 5.3 proves that repeated random entries succeed on one sampled threshold graph with probability tending to one. Finally, Section 5.4 accounts for complete scans and list overlaps to obtain matching bounds on distinct evaluations, even with a shared cache. 

5.1 Failure from a random entry 

We begin the restart analysis with a bound on the success probability of one run from a uniform random entry. The following theorem shows that this probability is exponentially small below the critical degree in both regimes. Its proof controls the number of expansions and the probability of finding a point of Top k(q) during a scan, providing the estimates used to determine the exact exponent in Section 5.2. Throughout this subsection the data are uniform. 

Theorem 5.2 (Random-entry failure) . Fix k ≥ 1 and suppose that the data are uniform. 

(a) In the logarithmic regime, fix 0 < β < β ∗(c) and let ∆ = ⌈nβ ⌉. There is η > 0 such that, with probability at least 1 − n−η over the data, a query set of surface measure at least 1 − n−η has the following property. For every query in this set, greedy navigation from a uniform random entry succeeds with probability at most n−η on either Gρβ or N∆ conditional on the sampled data, with probability taken over the entry label. 

54 (b) In the dense regime, fix 0 < γ < γ c and let ∆ = ⌈eγd ⌉. There is ζ > 0 such that, with probability at least 1 − e−ζd over the data, a query set of surface measure at least 1 − e−ζd has the following property. For every query in this set, greedy navigation from a uniform random entry succeeds with probability at most e−ζd on either Grγ or N∆ conditional on the sampled data, with probability taken over the entry label. 

The proof isolates a small cap around the query that contains the targets but has no improving edges. A run entering this cap at a nontarget point stops unsuccessfully. Success therefore requires either starting at a point of Top k(q) or revealing such a point in an expansion outside the cap. We bound the starting probability directly and control the probability of such an expansion by exposing only the caps scanned along the path and bounding the number of expansions. We first prove the bounds for Gρ, then transfer them to N∆ by comparing its neighbor-list radii with ρ.Let ρ = ρβ with 0 < β < β ∗(c) in the logarithmic regime, and ρ = rγ with 0 < γ < γ c in the dense regime. In both cases there is a fixed csub > 0 with sin ρ ≥ ecsub sin θ1 and 1 − cos ρ ≤ e−csub sin 2 θ1 (51) for all large n. In the logarithmic regime sin ρ/ sin θ1 → eβ/c by (6), and 1 − cos ρ < sin 2 θ1 in the limit is equivalent to ρ < ρ ∗, or β < β ∗(c). In the dense regime ρ/θ 1 → eγ ∈ (1 , √2). For a point x

with δ(q, x ) = θ, let U (x) be its improving region and u(θ) its surface measure. We also define a positive function Λ for θ1 ≤ θ ≤ π/ 2 by 

U (x) = C(x, ρ ) ∩ C(q, θ ), u(θ) = σd−1(U (x)) , and Λ( θ)2 = 2(1 − cos ρ) − (1 − cos ρ)2

sin 2 θ .

For every fixed ε > 0 and θ1 ≤ θ ≤ π/ 2 − ε, the ratio Λ( θ)/ sin θ1 determines the exponential scale of nu (θ) in (52). The expected number of improving neighbors is ( n − 1) u(θ). We use Ln = (log n)2

as a cutoff for nu (θ), bounding the probability of no improving neighbor above the cutoff and of very small moves below it. The search is analyzed through the following auxiliary exposure procedure. Fix the query q

and choose the entry x0 uniformly at random. The exploration reveals the position of x0 and then repeatedly expands the current point xi by revealing the unrevealed data points in C(xi, ρ ) and their positions. It moves to the sampled point of C(xi, ρ ) closest to q if that point is closer to q

than xi. It stops before expanding a current point in the trap cap specified after (53). Outside that cap, it stops after a complete expansion if it cannot move, or after Hn expansions, with Hn defined below. It ignores whether a point of Top k(q) has been revealed, and its stopping rule depends only on revealed positions. Until the exploration stops or the original greedy run succeeds, whichever occurs first, the two procedures visit the same points from x0, and every point evaluated by the original run up to that time has been revealed. The trap estimate below will rule out later success, while the path-length estimate will make truncation at Hn unlikely. Let Ei be the union of the caps revealed before the i-th expansion and Hi the revealed information at that time.

The function nu is continuous and nondecreasing on [ a, π/ 2 − ε] for a small fixed ε > 0, with nu (a) < 2Ln, while nu (π/ 2 − ε) is exponentially large because Λ( π/ 2 − ε) ≥ ecsub /2 sin θ1. For moves starting at distance at least θn, set hfar = A0/(4 d). For moves starting at distance in [ a, θ n), set 

hnear = A0/(64 dL n). Set Bn = ⌈θn/h near ⌉ + 2 and use expansion budget Hn = ⌈π/h far ⌉ + 2 Bn + 3. Since A0 and θn are within constant factors of θ1 in the dense regime and constants in the logarithmic regime, Hn = O(d/θ 1 + dL n). By (4) and log log n = o(d) this is eo(d) in both regimes. We show that, with probability at least 1 − ne −Ln/2, every data point x with δ(q, x ) ≥ θn has a data point y with 

δ(x, y ) ≤ ρ and δ(q, y ) ≤ δ(q, x ) − hfar . Let V (x) = C(x, ρ ) ∩ C(q, δ (q, x ) − hfar ). At δ(q, x ) = θn,the contraction ϕ used in the strip estimate with h = hfar sends U (x) into V (x) with Jacobian at least (1 − 1/(4 d)) d−1 ≥ 3/4, so σd−1(V (x)) ≥ 32 Ln/n . For θ = δ(q, x ) ∈ (θn, π ), this mass cannot decrease. Indeed, a point at distance t ≥ hfar from x lies in V (x) if and only if its directional cosine toward q is at least sin hfar / sin t + cot θ (cos hfar − cos t)/ sin t. This threshold is nonincreasing in 

θ because cos hfar − cos t ≥ 0. Points with t < h far cannot lie in V (x) by the triangle inequality, and θ = π follows by continuity of the intersection measure. Given a data point x, the other 

n − 1 points are independent and uniform, so the probability that none lies in V (x) is at most (1 − 32 Ln/n )n−1 ≤ e−Ln . A union bound over all n points proves the claim. To estimate success during the adaptive trajectory, we next determine the conditional law of the points not yet seen. Conditionally on Hi, the unrevealed data points are independent and uniform on Sd−1 \ Ei. Initially only x0 is revealed and the other n − 1 points are independent and uniform. If the claim holds before the i-th expansion, then the center xi is a function of Hi, and revealing which unrevealed points lie in C(xi, ρ ), with their positions, conditions each remaining unrevealed point, independently, on lying in Sd−1 \ (Ei ∪ C(xi, ρ )) = Sd−1 \ Ei+1 . Restricting a uniform law to a subset gives the uniform law on that subset. During the first Hn expansions, σd−1(Ei) ≤ HnF (ρ), which is nβ−1+ o(1) or eγd +o(d)/n , and in particular at most 1 /2 because d = o(log n) in the dense regime. Hence for every Borel set W , conditionally on Hi, the probability that some unrevealed point lies in W is at most n σ d−1(W )/(1 − σd−1(Ei)) ≤ 2n σ d−1(W ). Every point to which the exploration moves was unrevealed before that expansion. To see this, maintain the property that before expanding xi, every other revealed point is at least as far from q

57 as xi. Initially only x0 is revealed. If the exploration moves to xi+1 , its distance is smaller than that of xi, so it cannot be an earlier revealed point. Since xi+1 is the sampled point in the scanned cap closest to q, every point newly revealed in that expansion is at least as far from q as xi+1 . Thus the property holds before the next expansion. In the remaining interval [ a, θ n], there are few improving points, but (54) prevents many very small moves. We show that the exploration makes more than 2 Bn moves from points x with 

a ≤ δ(q, x ) ≤ θn with probability at most 4 −Bn . Call a move from xi slow if δ(q, x i) ∈ [a, θ n]and δ(q, x i+1 ) > δ (q, x i) − hnear . By the conditional exposure argument, a slow move requires an unrevealed point in the strip of (54) with h = hnear , whose mass is at most dh near A−10 u(θn) = 1 /(32 n). So, conditionally on Hi, the move is slow with probability at most 1 /16. Let ξj indicate that the j-th move made from a point at distance in [ a, θ n] is slow, with ξj = 0 if there is no such move. Suppose greedy navigation from x0 succeeds. If x0 ∈ Top k(q), call this a start in the target . Otherwise let xi /∈ Top k(q) be the point whose complete expansion first succeeds, and choose any y ∈ Gρ(xi) ∩ Top k(q). By (ii), y lies in the target cap. Since y is closer to q than xi and within distance ρ, property (i) puts xi outside the trap cap. Thus success from a nontarget entry requires an expansion outside the trap cap to reveal a point of the target cap, which we call a direct jump . A start in the target is covered by x0 ∈ C(q, b ), whose probability is 

F (b) = e−Ω( d). By (iii) and the conditional exposure argument, each expansion of a point outside the trap cap reveals a point of the target cap with conditional probability at most 2 e−cfail d, so a direct jump has probability at most 2 Hne−cfail d = e−Ω( d), because Hn = eo(d). The conditional estimates bound the exploration under the original sampling law. Adding the probabilities that the trap, target, or progress properties fail therefore gives, for every fixed q, navigation from a uniform entry succeeds with probability at most e−4ζd for a fixed ζ > 0, over the data and the entry. Gρ then give the stated data probability and exceptional surface measure for N∆. Intersecting the query sets and sample events for the two graphs, and decreasing ζ or η if necessary, makes the guarantees simultaneous for both graphs in each regime. 

5.2 Success probabilities and local exploration 

The bound in Theorem 5.2 establishes that success is rare, but the number of fresh entries needed depends on the exact success exponent. We determine this exponent in the degree ranges specified below and bound the success probability of local exploration in terms of its numbers of expansions and entry requests. The data are uniform and the query q is fixed. In the logarithmic regime let ∆ = ⌈nβ ⌉ with 0 < β < β ∗(c) and ρ = ρβ . In the dense regime let ∆ = ⌈eγd ⌉ with 0 < γ < γ c and 

ρ = rγ . Thus nF (ρ) = ∆(1 + o(1)), and we compare Gρ with N∆.We use the local exploration model introduced before Theorem 1.3, with a budget ( T, E ) of at most T complete expansions and E entry requests. A request evaluates its entry, whereas an expansion evaluates the complete neighbor list, including when it finds a target. Success is recorded after either operation if a point of Top k(q) has been evaluated. Thus local exploration may stop at a target entry without expanding it. A greedy run still follows the convention for WG and completes that entry’s expansion. In both cases, the cost counts distinct evaluated points across all operations. To state the success exponent, we first identify the distance from the query at which greedy progress becomes scarce. Write S = sin 2 θ1. For a point at query distance θ, the expected number (n − 1) u(θ) of improving neighbors on Gρ increases from exponentially small to exponentially large as θ crosses the zero-exponent boundary in (52). Denote this distance by θtrap , defined by sin 2 θtrap = (1 − cos ρ)2/(2(1 − cos ρ) − S). The inequalities in (51) place this distance strictly between θ1 and π/ 2. For a point whose distance from q is near θtrap , we first estimate the probability that its neighbor 59 list contains the nearest neighbor. For neighbor radius 0 < ϱ < π/ 2, consider a current point at query distance φ ∈ (0 , π ) and another point at query distance ψ ∈ (0 , π ). Their distance is at most 

ϱ exactly when the inner product of their unit tangent directions at q is at least wϱ(φ, ψ ). For independent uniform directions, pϱ(φ, ψ ) is the exponent of the probability of this edge in Gϱ. The spherical cosine identity gives wϱ, and we define the probability exponent pϱ by

We omit the superscript when ϱ = ρ.We determine the exact exponent of the single-run success probability in the near-threshold band , where a direct jump from the trap boundary is optimal. Theorem 5.3 (Single-start success and local exploration) . For uniform data in either regime, assume that ∆ lies in the near-threshold band and fix k ≥ 1. For each fixed query and each of Gρ and 

N∆, let p1 be the probability, over the data and a uniform random entry, that one greedy run succeeds on the chosen graph. On each graph, p1 = e−d(η∗+o(1)) . Every local exploration algorithm on that graph with budget (T, E ), T ≤ n/ (8∆) and E ≥ 1, succeeds with probability at most eo(d)(T + E)p1

over the data and its internal randomness. The o(d) term is independent of the algorithm and the budgets T, E .

To upper-bound both single-run and local-exploration success, we group revealed points by their distances from q and bound the expected number of routes leading to a target. For the matching lower bound, we hide the nearest neighbor’s direction and construct a greedy path that stops within a small enlargement of the trap radius. We then use the independent hidden direction to bound the probability that this stopping point lists the nearest neighbor. The geometric comparison needed for the upper bound comes first. To bound success along a route with intermediate points, edge probability alone is insufficient because an expansion may reveal many candidates at each distance. Define ν(ψ) = log(sin ψ/ sin θ1). For ψ in the angular range of (5), ν(ψ) gives the exponent in d of the expected number of points in 

C(q, ψ ). Adding ν(ψ) to the edge-probability exponent pϱ(φ, ψ ) gives the transition exponent gϱ for the expected number of neighbors. Its symmetric version χϱ assigns half the counting exponent to each endpoint. The difference between gϱ and χϱ then telescopes along a route. These rates satisfy

λ − ν(θtrap ) = − log sin θtrap . The value of a chain that starts inside the trap cap is at most 0, because all its terms χ are nonpositive and sin θ1 ≤ sin φ0. So ηentry ≥ − log sin θtrap . By the derivative formula in part (b), with the roles of the arguments exchanged, ∂ψw(θtrap , ψ ) has the sign of cos θtrap − a cos ψ ≥ a(1 − cos ψ) ≥ 0, so w(θtrap , θ 1) ≤ w(θtrap , θ trap ) = 1 − (1 − a)/ sin 2 θtrap =(S −(1 −a)) /(1 −a). ν(θtrap ). The preceding entry-exponent comparison shows that ∂ψw(θtrap , ψ ) > 0 for 0 < ψ ≤ θtrap ,so 0 < w (θtrap , θ 1) < w (θtrap , θ trap ) and η∗ < ν (θtrap ). In the dense regime, ∂yw(L, y ) = ( y2 − L2 +

M 2)/(2 Ly 2) > 0 because L ≤ M . Thus 0 < w (L, 1) < w (L, L ), while the limiting diagonal identity gives −p(L, L ) = ν(L) = log L. It follows that η∗ < ν (L) as required. The geometric rates can now be applied to an adaptive exploration. To preserve independence of the unrevealed points, expose a cap containing the entire neighbor list at each expansion. Define ρ+

by nF (ρ+) = 2∆, and retain R± from the proof of Theorem 5.2, where nF (R±) = ∆(1 ± εn) and 

εn = (log n/ ∆) 1/4. These radii differ from ρ by a relative O(1 /d ), so (51) still holds with a smaller fixed margin. For each data point x, its list in Gρ is determined by the points in C(x, ρ +). For N∆

this is true whenever every such cap contains at least ∆ other data points. Denote that data event by C. Chernoff’s inequality gives P(Cc) ≤ ne −∆/4, whereas for Gρ we take C to be the whole sample space. In the auxiliary exposure procedure, an entry request reveals that point’s position, and an expansion reveals the complete cap C(x, ρ +). The algorithm still evaluates only its requested entry or scanned neighbor list. For N∆, form the list from the ∆ nearest other exposed points in that cap, and scan all available points and stop if there are fewer than ∆. Complete every cap exposure and list scan, including one that triggers a stop. On C, these lists are the true lists. The next theorem bounds success using this auxiliary procedure.

. All three terms are smaller than every fixed negative power of n, since ∆ ≥ (log n)K for every fixed positive power when log log n = o(d). A run has one entry, but its expansion count is random. If the entry already lies in this cap, its full expansion is still completed. We have Hn∆/n = eo(d)∆/n → 0, so Hn ≤ n/ (8∆) for all large n. The containing caps exposed during these expansions have total surface measure at most 

HnF (ρ+) = o(1). The auxiliary process in the proof of Theorem 5.5 stops at the first operation that reveals any point of C(q, b 0). This can precede an actual evaluation in the cap, since exposure also reveals points outside the neighbor list. On C, its scans and moves agree with those of the truncated run until this auxiliary stop. A stop triggered by an expansion completes that entire scan and exposure. A stop triggered by the entry request precedes the entry expansion of the actual run. In either case, the expansions included in Tnear form a subset of those of the truncated run, so 

ETnear ≤ O(dL n) + nϖ n + HnP(Cc) = eo(d).On C and Top k(q) ⊆ C(q, b 0), success of the original run before the budget is exhausted requires the auxiliary process to reveal at least one point of C(q, b 0). Thus the reduction in (58) applies to the original run, with an additional error ϖn for exhausting the budget before a target-cap evaluation. The proof of Theorem 5.5 bounds e0, the expected number of target-cap points revealed through the auxiliary stop, by the expansion and entry terms. We use that bound with the preceding estimate on ETnear . By Theorem 5.4(c), ηgen = η∗ and, in the logarithmic regime, ηentry > η ∗. In the dense regime, the entry term instead satisfies eCd /n = e−ω(d). The error terms P(Cc), exp( −ec′d), and 

ϖn are smaller than every fixed negative power of n, and hence than e−d(η∗+1) . Therefore, for all sufficiently large n,

p1 ≤ eo(d)e−d(η∗−ε/ 2) + e−d(η∗−ε/ 2) + P(Cc) + exp( −ec′d) + ϖn ≤ e−d(η∗−ε).

Together with the preceding lower bound, this gives p1 = e−d(η∗+o(1)) . Finally, expressing the terms in the bound of Theorem 5.5 as multiples of p1 ≥ e−d(η∗+ε), and absorbing its additive terms into 

Ep 1 since E ≥ 1, gives a success probability of at most e3εd (T + E) p1. Since ε > 0 is arbitrary, this is eo(d)(T + E) p1.

5.3 Independent restarts 

Theorem 5.3 gives a success probability averaged over the sample and the entry. Restarts reuse one sample, so that average alone does not determine how many attempts suffice. We now show that, with probability 1 − e−Ω( d) over the sample, the fraction of successful entries on Gρ differs from the single-start probability by at most a factor e±εd for any fixed ε > 0. Conditional on the sample, independent attempts then succeed using a number of entries whose leading exponential scale is the reciprocal of the single-run success probability. 

Theorem 5.6 (Restarts) . For uniform data in either regime, take k = 1 in the success rule of Section 1.2, assume that ∆ lies in the near-threshold band defined in Section 5.2, and let ε > 0. Thus a run succeeds after its first completed expansion whose evaluated points include the nearest neighbor. There is cε > 0 such that for all large n, every query q fixed before sampling, and every integer 

B ≥ e(η∗+ε)d, B independent runs of greedy navigation on Gρ from uniformly random entries, drawn with replacement, all fail to evaluate the nearest neighbor of q with probability at most e−cεd over the data and restart entries. Moreover, with probability at least 1−e−cεd over the data, the fraction SD(q)

of entries from which the run evaluates the nearest neighbor satisfies e−(η∗+ε)d ≤ SD(q) ≤ e−(η∗−ε)d.The constant cε depends only on ε and on (c, β ) in the logarithmic regime or γ in the dense regime, and the bounds are uniform in q.

69 To obtain a lower bound on the fraction of successful entries on one sample, we compare two runs from independent random entries. We restrict the comparison to blind paths with at most 

eo(d) vertices that stay outside a cap centered at q with radius slightly below θtrap . In the sample with the nearest neighbor removed, points whose labels are not on a fixed path must avoid the part of each nonterminal point’s neighbor cap closer to q than the chosen successor, and all improving locations in the final point’s neighbor cap. We bound the probability mass of the union of these regions. The bounds on path length, distance from q, and excluded probability mass let us show that the fraction of entries satisfying those bounds and succeeding on the original graph has small variance. Resampling after fixing the first path produces a second sample that is independent of that path. We then compare the second trajectory on the original and resampled samples. The resulting error in the joint success probability of the first and second runs requires both a change in the second trajectory and the hidden nearest neighbor to lie within radius ρ of a vertex on the first path. 

Proof. For the resampling comparison, we retain paths whose vertices stay outside the interior of a cap slightly smaller than C(q, θ trap ). Let A = θtrap − ι in the logarithmic regime and A = ( L − ι)θ1

in the dense regime, where the positive constants ι, ι ′, ι 1 will be chosen in that order. Write νtrap for the limiting exponent of the expected number of points within radius θtrap , namely lim n→∞ ν(θtrap )in the logarithmic regime and log L in the dense regime. The key comparison is η∗ < ν trap from Theorem 5.4(c). For the paths retained in the comparison, we bound the probability that resampling changes the second path by a quantity with decay exponent approaching νtrap . The joint success error also requires the hidden target direction to make the first path succeed. Conditional on both samples with the target removed and on the entry labels, this direction is independent and uniform, giving a further probability bound with decay exponent approaching η∗. The sum of the two decay exponents exceeds 2 η∗, allowing the joint success error to be smaller than the square of the single-run success probability. To complete Theorem 5.1, we must convert the success and expansion bounds into bounds on distinct evaluations. We first bound the total cost of independent restarts on Grγ , including failed attempts and the final complete expansion. For the lower bound, we combine the local exploration probability bound of Theorem 5.5 with a bound on overlaps between scanned neighbor lists. This gives a lower bound on distinct evaluations even when all attempts share a cache. 

Corollary 5.7 (Evaluation cost of independent restarts) . For uniform data in the dense regime, let 

∆ = ⌈eγd ⌉ lie in the near-threshold band, 12 log 43 ≤ γ < γ c, and let ε > 0. On Grγ , take k = 1 in the success rule as in Theorem 5.6 and let B = ⌈e(η∗+ε)d⌉ independent runs start from uniformly random entries. With probability 1 − e−Ω( d) over the data and the B entry labels, they evaluate the nearest neighbor of a fixed query, and the total number of distance evaluations performed by the runs is at most e(γ+η∗(γ)+ ε+o(1)) d. The cost includes every attempt and its final complete expansion, whether that attempt succeeds or stops unsuccessfully. The achieved exponent γ + η∗(γ) is minimized over this band at e2γ = (12 + 2 √3) /11 , where its value is approximately 0.243 .Proof. The success assertion follows from Theorem 5.6 with ε/ 2. For the evaluation cost, we bound the number of complete expansions made from every entry until success or termination. Fix the query, put a = θ1/2, and retain Ln = (log n)2. By (5), nF (a) = exp {(− log 2 + o(1)) d}. Thus, except with probability e−(log 2+ o(1)) d, the data contain no point of C(q, a ). Apply (54) and the bounds on moves above θn and slow moves in [ a, θ n] from the proof of Theorem 5.2, using this a. That proof requires a to be within fixed factors of θ1 and to satisfy nu (a) < 2Ln. Both conditions hold because 

a is a fixed multiple of θ1 and nu (a) ≤ nF (a) < 2Ln. The monotonicity of u and its growth above the trap boundary also give a fixed query distance below π/ 2 at which nu exceeds 2 Ln for all large 

n. With A0 = 14 min {rγ , a }, those arguments give an expansion budget Hn = O(d/θ 1 + dL n) = eo(d)

and a non-slow-move budget Bn = Θ( dL n). A run from a uniform entry, stopped on entering C(q, a ), exceeds Hn expansions with probability at most ϖn = ne −Ln/2 + 4 −Bn . The same bound holds for each fixed entry label by exchangeability. A union bound over the n labels gives failure probability at most nϖ n = e−ω(d). When C(q, a ) is empty, stopping at this cap is impossible. The expansion bound therefore applies to every complete run, including its final expansion, on a data event of probability 1 − e−Ω( d). We next bound the number of points evaluated by one complete expansion. Conditional on one source, its neighbor-list length is binomial with mean at most eγd . Chernoff and a union over the labels give max x dGrγ (x) ≤ 2eγd outside an event of probability n exp {− ce γd } = e−ω(d) for some fixed c > 0. Each run therefore performs at most 1 + 2 Hneγd = e(γ+o(1)) d evaluations. Summing over the B runs proves the cost bound, even with separate caches. A shared cache can only reduce this total. Theorem 5.9 (Distinct-evaluation lower bound in the near-threshold band) . For uniform data in the dense regime, fix k ≥ 1, 12 log(4 /3) ≤ γ < γ c, and ε > 0, and put ∆ = ⌈eγd ⌉ and η = η∗(γ). On either Grγ or N∆, consider any measurable local exploration algorithm in the model of Section 5.2, without an imposed expansion or entry-request budget. For each fixed query q, let V be the number of distinct query–data distance evaluations through the first completed expansion or entry request that evaluates a point of Top k(q), using the local exploration convention in Section 5.2. If the algorithm finds no target, set V = ∞. Then for all sufficiently large n,

P{V ≤ e(γ+η−ε)d} ≤ e−cεd, (62) 

where cε = min {ε, η/ 2}/16 > 0. The probability is over the data and the algorithm’s randomness. Constants and the threshold for n are uniform in the fixed query and the local exploration algorithm. Caches may be shared throughout the search. Every expansion scans the complete neighbor list, including an expansion that finds a target. Proof. The union estimate applies to sets of expanded points of size eτ d with τ < J ov (γ). To reach the single-start exponent, it suffices to check η ≤ Jov (γ). Retain Eγ = e2γ ∈ [4 /3, 2) and the expression for w(L, 1) from the proof of Theorem 5.7, so η = − 12 log(1 − w(L, 1) 2). The band identity of Theorem 5.4(c) identifies this η with ηgen . Direct algebra gives 14Eγ

− w(L, 1) 2 = (3 Eγ − 4) {4 − 3Eγ (Eγ − 2) 2}

> γ

(Eγ − 1) ≥ 0.

Indeed 3 Eγ − 4 ≥ 0 and 3 Eγ (Eγ − 2) 2 ≤ 3 · 2 · (2 /3) 2 = 8 /3 < 4. Since t 7 → − 12 log(1 − t) is increasing, we obtain η ≤ Jov (γ), with equality only at Eγ = 4 /3. 78 We choose an expansion limit small enough for Theorem 5.8 to apply, but large enough that completing that many expansions would exceed the evaluation budget. It suffices to use min {ε, η/ 2}

in place of ε. Choose cwork = min {ε, η/ 2}/8 and τ = η − 2cwork , and set Mn = ⌊eτ d ⌋. Use the distinct-evaluation budget ⌊e(γ+η−min {ε,η/ 2})d⌋. Here Mn limits the number of distinct expansions. Since the evaluation cost is integer-valued, the event that it is at most e(γ+η−ε)d is contained in the event that it is at most this integer budget. Since 0 < τ < J ov (γ), including at the lower band endpoint, apply Theorem 5.8 with this τ . Its constants are chosen next by the explicit formulas in its proof. Finally apply Theorem 5.5 with exponent slack cwork , choosing its constants in the order specified there, and only then take n sufficiently large. Before applying the expansion bound, we remove entry requests whose distances are already in the actual algorithm’s exploration history. Such requests can be simulated internally, preserving the original algorithm’s random choices and subsequent decisions. The model permits each neighbor list to be expanded only once, so we count every expansion. Among entry requests, count only those that evaluate a previously unevaluated distance. To apply the local-exploration estimate, we must also distinguish actual distance evaluations from the additional coordinate information exposed in its proof. Conditional on that exposed information, each label whose position has not yet been exposed, and which is selected by a data-independent entry rule from the local exploration model, is uniform on the unexposed region. Requesting an already exposed label adds no newly revealed point to the counts in (59). If such a label lies in C(q, b 0) from the proof of Theorem 5.5, it was already included at first exposure in e0, the expected number of revealed points in that cap. An entry whose position has not been exposed also has an unevaluated distance, so the number of requests contributing to the entry term is at most the number of first distance evaluations through entry requests. On En, completing Mn distinct expansions requires at least ∆Mn

4d ≥ e(γ+η−2cwork )d

8d > ⌊e(γ+η−min {ε,η/ 2})d⌋

distinct evaluations for all sufficiently large n. All points in the union of these completed neighbor lists have actually been evaluated, regardless of overlap with previous neighbor lists or entries. Hence an exploration whose first target is found within this budget has fewer than Mn completed expansions, including the final expansion, and at most ⌊e(γ+η−min {ε,η/ 2})d⌋ entry requests that evaluate a previously unevaluated distance. Consequently, apart from Ecn, such success is contained in the success event of the simulated algorithm stopped after Mn complete expansions or ⌊e(γ+η−min {ε,η/ 2})d⌋ entry requests that evaluate new distances. It obeys the required expansion bound Mn ≤ n/ (8∆) for all large n, since log n/d →∞. The removal of redundant operations above justifies applying the proof of Theorem 5.5 with these budgets, including for an original algorithm that makes redundant requests. Applying the conditional exposure estimate and recursion in the proof of Theorem 5.5, together with Theorem 5.4(c) and Tnear ≤ Mn, bounds the stopped simulation’s success probability by

n + P(Cc) + exp {− ec′d}.

Here C, c ′ > 0 depend only on γ, ε, and k, and C is the event that every exposure cap C(x, ρ +)contains at least ∆ other data points, as in Theorem 5.5. For Grγ take C to be the whole sample space, so P(Cc) = 0, while for N∆, P(Cc) ≤ ne −∆/4. The first term is at most e−cwork d. The entry term is e−ω(d), because the entry-request budget is eO(d) whereas log n/d → ∞ . The other terms in the displayed success bound are e−ω(d). Adding P(Ecn) bounds the original event of success within the 79 stated integer evaluation budget, and this additional term is also e−ω(d).
