q.

Proof of Theorem 5.1. For each fixed γ in the stated band, Theorem 5.9 gives the distinct-evaluation lower bound on both Grγ and N∆. By Theorem 5.7, independent restarts with k = 1 attain the matching upper exponent on Grγ with probability tending to one, counting all failed attempts and complete expansions. To obtain the upper bound for any fixed k ≥ 1, use the same sample and entry sequence for both procedures. Before the first completed expansion after which the evaluated labels include a point of Top k(q), their failed attempts, successors, and shared caches coincide. Both procedures complete that expansion, including when its entry is already a target. The fixed-k procedure therefore succeeds no later, and its evaluated labels form a subset of those evaluated by the k = 1 procedure within the same number of attempts prescribed by Theorem 5.7. This transfers both success and the distinct-evaluation bound to fixed k. The estimates hold for every fixed ε > 0 in Theorems 5.7 and 5.9. Letting ε tend to zero sufficiently slowly gives the o(1) terms in Theorem 5.1. The minimization performed in the proof of Theorem 5.7 gives the value approximately 0 .243, while Theorems 3.1 and 3.4 give the every-entry exponent γc ≈ 0.347. 

5.5 The rank of the stopping point 

After determining the cost of exact recovery with fresh entries, we ask what rank greedy navigation can guarantee from every entry if exact recovery is relaxed. Throughout this subsection, a run continues until no listed neighbor is closer to the query than its current point. For uniform data below the critical degree, the lens estimates of Section 2 and the direction comparison in Section 5.1 locate a cap outside which every data point has an improving neighbor. For nonuniform data, the progress bounds of Section 4 provide such a cap over a larger range of density variation than exact recovery allows. In each case, every full run stops inside the cap, and the number of sampled points in that cap bounds the stopping rank. 

Corollary 5.10 (Stopping rank for uniform data) . For uniform data in the dense regime, fix 

0 < γ < γ c and ε > 0, and let ∆ = ⌈eγd ⌉. Define νγ = 12 log e4γ  

> 4( e2γ−1)

. With probability 1 − o(1) over the data, every query outside a set of surface measure e−Ω( d) has the following property. From every entry, the full greedy run on either Grγ or N∆ stops at a point of rank at most e(νγ +ε)d.Proof. To find a cap outside which every point has a closer neighbor, write Lg = e2g/(2 √e2g − 1), so that log Lγ = νγ . Choose 0 < g < γ close enough to γ that log Lg < ν γ + ε/ 8, and then choose 

A > L g with log A < ν γ + ε/ 4. Set ρ = rg and θ0 = Aθ 1. The graph Gρ is contained in Grγ . It is also contained in N∆ with probability 1 − exp( −eΩ( d)), since each radius-ρ cap contains at most egd 

points in expectation and g < γ . This follows from (9) and a union bound over the sources. For a source x at distance θ from a fixed query q, let uρ(θ) = σd−1(C(x, ρ ) ∩ C(q, θ )) be the mass of its improving region. Since ρ/θ 1 → eg and θ0/θ 1 = A, Theorems 2.1 and 2.2 give 1

d log  nu ρ(θ0) = 12 log 



e2g − e4g

4A2



+ o(1) > 0.

The strict inequality is exactly A > L g. The error is o(1) because log(1 /θ 1) = o(d). Moreover, 

uρ(θ) is nondecreasing in θ. Indeed, for a point at distance t ≤ ρ from x, the spherical cosine identity makes improvement equivalent to a directional cosine larger than cot θ tan( t/ 2), which decreases with θ. Thus the improving region of every source at distance at least θ0 contains eΩ( d)

80 points in expectation. Conditional on the source, the remaining points are independent. The probability that any of the n sources in this range lacks an improving neighbor is therefore at most 

n exp( −eΩ( d)) = exp( −eΩ( d)). Combining this estimate with retention of Gρ shows that every full run stops inside C(q, Aθ 1). Its stopping rank is at most the number of data points in that cap. By (5), this number has mean 

e(log A+o(1)) d, and (9) bounds it by e(νγ +ε)d except with probability exp( −eΩ( d)). The estimates are uniform in the fixed query. Integrating over surface measure and applying Markov’s inequality gives one data event of probability 1 − o(1) and an exceptional query set of measure e−Ω( d). Distance ties are excluded as in the navigation conventions. For nonuniform data, the progress estimates can hold even when the inner cap contains false local minima. This gives a wider density range for bounded stopping rank than for exact recovery. 

Corollary 5.11 (Stopping rank for nonuniform data) . In the dense regime, fix γc < γ ≤ log 2 ,

0 ≤ κ < γ − γc, and ε > 0, and let ∆ = ⌈eγd ⌉. For every sufficiently large fixed K depending on γ

and κ, let f be (κ, K )-regular and let Q be an absolutely continuous query distribution, both fixed before the data. With probability 1 − e−ω(d) over the data, there is a query set of Q-probability at least 

1 − e−ω(d) on which every full greedy run on N∆ from every entry stops within O(1 + ρ−1min ) = eo(d)

moves at a point of rank at most 2e(κ+ε)d.Proof. Regularity gives f > 0 almost everywhere, as shown in the proof of Theorem 4.1(a), and every query satisfies the local density condition with parameter κ. Choose g with κ + γc < g < γ ,then choose a with eκ−g < a < min {1/√2, e κ+ε−g}. Put A = g + log a < κ + ε. Use the radius comparisons, retention, and outer progress estimates in the proof of Theorem 4.3 with ¯ κ = κ1 = κ and with K at least K0 recomputed from the formula in that proof using the present g and ¯ κ = κ1 = κ.Their condition g > ¯κ holds throughout the present range. The middle region estimate in that proof applies with the positive margins A − κ and g − κ − γc, yielding a factor b < 1 and an expected number eΩ( d) of neighbors that reduce the query distance by this factor. Thus, for each fixed query, every run reaches C(q, Θ), where R = Rq is defined in that proof and Θ = arcsin( a sin R), except with probability e−ω(d).The path-length argument in that proof uses only retention of the radius-R edges and a < 1/√2, so it also bounds full runs that stop at a false local minimum. Every run stops inside this cap within O(1 + ρ−1min ) moves. Using Mq from the same proof, the number of sampled points in the cap is binomial with mean at most nM qF (Θ) ≤ a−1eAd , and hence at most 2 a−1eAd ≤ 2e(κ+ε)d except with doubly exponentially small probability, by (9). Integration against Q and Markov’s inequality give the common query set and the stated probability bounds. The stopping rank guarantee can hold for the same sampling density and query distribution that force failing entries for every graph of degree at most ∆. For any fixed ε > 0, γc < γ ≤ log 2, and h(γ) < κ < γ − γc, the density and query distribution Qn in Theorem 4.1(b) also meet the hypotheses of Theorem 5.11, since the density is ( κ, K )-regular for every fixed K > 0. With probability 1 − e−Ω( d) over the sample, every graph G of degree at most ∆ has a query set of 

Qn-probability at least 1 − e−Ω( d) obtained by intersecting the query sets from Theorem 4.1(b) and Theorem 5.11. For every query in this intersection, greedy navigation on G fails from eΩ( d) entries and every full greedy run on N∆ from every entry has stopping rank at most 2 e(κ+ε)d.81 AI methodologies 

OpenAI ChatGPT and Codex were used during the mathematical development of this work. In particular, initial versions of the hard-instance constructions used in the proof of Theorem 4.1(b) and in Theorem 4.8 were developed with ChatGPT. ChatGPT also assisted in checking the exponent identities and the compatibility of the parameter inequalities in these two constructions, as well as the minimization of the evaluation cost exponent for independent restarts in Theorem 5.1 (see the proof of Theorem 5.7). The author rewrote every argument retained in the paper, independently verified the correctness and originality of all content, including references, and takes full responsibility for the final manuscript.
